Ore localization of amenable monoid actions and applications towards entropy – addition formulas and the bridge theorem
Dikran DikranjanAnna Giordano BrunoSimone Virili

Abstract
For a left action S ​ λ ↷ X S\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 6.0pt$\lambda$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}X of a cancellative right amenable monoid S S on a discrete Abelian group X X , we construct its Ore localization G ​ λ ∗ ↷ X ∗ G\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 6.0pt$\lambda^{*}$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}X^{*} , where G G is the group of left fractions of S S ; analogously, for a right action K ​ ρ ↶ S K\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 7.0pt$\rho$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowleft\ $}}S on a compact space K K , we construct its Ore colocalization K ∗ ​ ρ ∗ ↶ G K^{*}\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 7.0pt$\rho^{*}$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowleft\ $}}G . Both constructions preserve entropy, i.e., for the algebraic entropy h alg h_{\mathrm{alg}} and for the topological entropy h top h_{\mathrm{top}} one has h alg ​ ( λ ) = h alg ​ ( λ ∗ ) h_{\mathrm{alg}}(\lambda)=h_{\mathrm{alg}}(\lambda^{*}) and h top ​ ( ρ ) = h top ​ ( ρ ∗ ) h_{\mathrm{top}}(\rho)=h_{\mathrm{top}}(\rho^{*}) , respectively. Exploiting these constructions and the theory of quasi-tilings, we extend the Addition Theorem for h top h_{\mathrm{top}} , known for right actions of countable amenable groups on compact metrizable groups [ 36 ] , to right actions K ​ ρ ↶ S K\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 7.0pt$\rho$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowleft\ $}}S of cancellative right amenable monoids S S (with no restrictions on the cardinality) on arbitrary compact groups K K . When the compact group K K is Abelian, we prove that h top ​ ( ρ ) h_{\mathrm{top}}(\rho) coincides with h alg ​ ( ρ ∧ ) h_{\mathrm{alg}}({\rho}^{\wedge}) , where S ​ ρ ∧ ↷ X S\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 6.0pt${\rho}^{\wedge}$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}X is the dual left action on the discrete Pontryagin dual X = K ∧ X={K}^{\wedge} , that is, a so-called Bridge Theorem. From the Addition Theorem for h top h_{\mathrm{top}} and the Bridge Theorem, we obtain an Addition Theorem for h alg h_{\mathrm{alg}} for left actions S ​ λ ↷ X S\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 6.0pt$\lambda$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}X on discrete Abelian groups, so far known only under the hypotheses that either X X is torsion [ 10 ] or S S is locally monotileable [ 11 ] . The proofs substantially use the unified approach towards entropy based on the entropy of actions of cancellative right amenable monoids on appropriately defined normed monoids, as developed in [ 17 ] (for ℕ {\mathbb{N}} -actions) and [ 59 ] .



Keywords: amenable monoid action, topological entropy, algebraic entropy, Ore (co)localization,
addition theorem, bridge theorem.

MSC2020. Primary: 18A40, 20K30, 37B40, 54C70, 54H15. Secondary: 18F60, 20K40, 20M15,20M20, 22D35, 43A07, 54B30.

Introduction

The notion of
topological entropy

h_{\mathrm{top}}
was first introduced by Adler, Konheim and McAndrew
[
1
]
in 1965 as an invariant of continuous self-maps of compact topological spaces; we denote by
{\mathbf{CompSp}}
(resp.,
\mathbf{CompGr}
) the category of compact Hausdorff spaces (resp., groups) and continuous maps (resp., homomorphisms) and in this paper when we write compact space (resp., group) we mean compact Hausdorff space (resp., group). Few years later, Bowen
[
4
]
introduced a notion of entropy for uniformly continuous self-maps of metric spaces (see also
[
21
]
), later extended to general uniform spaces by Hood
[
33
]
. For a continuous self-map
f\colon K\to K
of a compact space
K
, both Hood’s extension of Bowen’s entropy (taking
K
with the unique compatible uniformity) and the original (Adler, Konheim and McAndrew’s) notion of topological entropy coincide (see
[
20
,
28
]
or Example
1.24
for details).

Let
S=(S,\cdot,{1})
be a monoid,
K
a compact space, and denote by
\mathrm{End}_{{\mathbf{CompSp}}}(K)
the monoid of continuous self-maps of
K
.
A
right
S
-action

K\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 7.0pt$\rho$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowleft\ $}}S

is a monoid anti-homomorphism
\rho\colon S\to\mathrm{End}_{{\mathbf{CompSp}}}(K)
, that is, letting, for each
s\in S
,
\rho_{s}=\rho(s)\in\mathrm{End}_{{\mathbf{CompSp}}}(K)
,

(RA.1)

\rho_{1}=\mathrm{id}_{K}
;

(RA.2)

\rho_{st}=\rho_{t}\circ\rho_{s}
, for all
s,t\in S
.

If
K
is a compact group, we require that the monoid anti-homomorphism
\rho
takes values in
\mathrm{End}_{\mathbf{CompGr}}(K)\subseteq\mathrm{End}_{\mathbf{CompSp}}(K)
, that is, we want each
\rho_{s}
to be a continuous endomorphism of
K
. A continuous self-map
f\colon K\to K
of a compact space
K
(respectively, a continuous endomorphism of a compact group
K
) uniquely identifies a right action
\rho
of the additive monoid
{\mathbb{N}}=({\mathbb{N}},+,0)
on
K
by setting
\rho(1)=f
(and so
\rho(n)=f^{n}
for every
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, with the usual convention that
f^{0}=\mathrm{id}_{K}
).
Using this terminology, the topological entropy can be seen as an invariant of the right
{\mathbb{N}}
-actions on compact spaces (respectively, compact groups).

Of course, there is no reason to confine the theory of topological entropy to actions of the naturals. In fact, already in the 1980s, several authors developed a theory of topological entropy for actions of amenable groups (see, for example,
[
46
,
47
,
56
]
and, for more recent results,
[
7
,
9
,
36
]
). A recent breakthrough is the extension of this entropy theory to actions of sofic groups by Bowen
[
5
]
(see also
[
37
,
62
]
).

A foundational result for the theory of topological entropy of amenable group actions is the so-called Ornstein-Weiss Lemma.
Roughly speaking, to define the topological entropy one has to study certain nets of real numbers (see §
1
for details); Lindenstrauss and Weiss
[
41
]
(see also
[
35
]
for a different approach based on an idea from
[
30
]
) proved, using the theory of quasi-tilings of Ornstein and Weiss
[
47
]
, that, for amenable group actions, all these nets converge. Recently, Ceccherini-Silberstein, Coornaert and Krieger
[
6
]
were able to extend this convergence result to include any right action
K\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 7.0pt$\rho$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowleft\ $}}S
of a cancellative and right amenable monoid
S
on a compact space
K
, setting the bases for the study of the topological entropy of such right
S
-actions (see §
1.6
). We denote by
h_{\mathrm{top}}(\rho)
the topological entropy of
\rho
, keeping the same notation of the case of right
{\mathbb{N}}
-actions (i.e., those induced by single continuous self-maps).

Any amenable group
G
is, trivially, also a cancellative and right amenable monoid. On the other hand, any cancellative and right amenable monoid
S
is a
left Ore monoid
and, therefore, it has a (necessarily amenable) group of left fractions
G=S^{-1}S
(see §
1.2
). In other words, there is a natural (i.e., canonical, and even functorial) way to assign an amenable group
G
to any cancellative and right amenable monoid
S
. We will see in §
3
that, in fact, the formation of the group of left fractions can be extended to right actions on compact spaces via the construction of the so-called
Ore colocalization
of a right
S
-action
K\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 7.0pt$\rho$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowleft\ $}}S
on a compact space
K
, which is a right
G
-action
K^{*}\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 7.0pt$\rho^{*}$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowleft\ $}}G
of the group of left fractions of
S
(so necessarily an action by homeomorphisms) on a new compact space
K^{*}
; such colocalization can be thought of (in a sense that can be made precise) as the right
G
-action that “best approximates”
K\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 7.0pt$\rho$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowleft\ $}}S
. More precisely,
K^{*}\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 7.0pt$\rho^{*}$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowleft\ $}}G
is the result of the following two successive modifications of
K\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 7.0pt$\rho$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowleft\ $}}S
. First, one restricts
\rho
to a closed invariant subspace
\bar{K}
of
K
, called the
surjective core
of
\rho
, to get a right
S
-action
\bar{K}\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 7.0pt$\bar{\rho}$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowleft\ $}}S
by surjective continuous self-maps. This first construction already satisfies the following universal property: for any right
S
-action
K^{\prime}\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 7.0pt$\rho^{\prime}$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowleft\ $}}S
on a compact space
K^{\prime}
by surjective continuous self-maps, any
S
-equivariant continuous map
f\colon K^{\prime}\to K
factors uniquely through the inclusion
\bar{K}\to K
.
The second step then consists in building a surjective inverse system
\mathfrak{K}
of copies of
\bar{K}
, with transition maps suitably induced by
\bar{\rho}
; the inverse limit
K^{*}
of
\mathfrak{K}
admits a canonical right
S
-action
K^{*}\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 7.0pt$$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowleft\ $}}S
by homeomorphisms, which is characterized by an analogous universal property among the right
S
-actions by homeomorphisms. This
S
-action extends uniquely to a right
G
-action
K^{*}\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 7.0pt$\rho^{*}$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowleft\ $}}G
: the Ore colocalization of
K\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 7.0pt$\rho$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowleft\ $}}G
.
The above construction of the restricted action on the surjective core and of the Ore colocalization are remarkably well-behaved for several reasons: first, they can be directly applied (with no modification required) also to actions on compact (Abelian) groups by continuous endomorphisms, they are functorial and they both preserve the injectivity and surjectivity of equivariant morphisms. Finally and, perhaps, most importantly: they both preserve the topological entropy, that is, in the above notation,
h_{\mathrm{top}}(\rho)=h_{\mathrm{top}}(\bar{\rho})=h_{\mathrm{top}}(\rho^{*})
.

In the sequel we discuss a deep property of the topological entropy. To this end we recall first that, for a right
S
-action
K\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 7.0pt$\rho$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowleft\ $}}S
on a compact group
K
and an
S
-invariant (not necessarily normal) subgroup
H
of
K
, one can consider the two induced right
S
-actions

H\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 7.0pt$\rho_{H}$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowleft\ $}}S
and
K/H\hskip 5.0pt\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{6.02773pt}{\hskip-2.5pt$\rho_{K/H}$}}}{\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\ \curvearrowleft\ $}}\hskip 6.0ptS
,
where
\rho_{H}
is the restriction of
\rho
to
H
and
\rho_{K/H}
is the action induced by
\rho
on the compact space
K/H
of left
H
-cosets in
K
.
The equality

h_{\mathrm{top}}(\rho)=h_{\mathrm{top}}(\rho_{H})+h_{\mathrm{top}}(\rho_{K/H})

[formula]

is known as Addition Theorem. It was established by Li
[
36
]
(see also
[
37
,
38
]
) when
S
is a countable amenable group and
K
is a compact metrizable group;
in fact, this is a direct consequence of
[
36
, Theorem 6.1]
(see also
[
37
, Theorem 13.47]
, including the brief discussion introducing the result).

The first of our main results is the following extension of Li’s Addition Theorem, in which we prove (
at
top
)
for right
S
-actions on compact groups
K
. Therefore, our (
at
top
) extends Li’s Addition Theorem in three
different directions: first,
S
is allowed to be non-countable, second, we pass from actions of amenable groups to actions of cancellative right amenable monoids, and third, the action is on compact groups that are not necessarily metrizable.

Topological Addition Theorem.

Let
S
be a cancellative and right amenable monoid,
K
a compact group,
K\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 7.0pt$\rho$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowleft\ $}}S
a right
S
-action and
H
a closed
S
-invariant subgroup of
K
. Then
(\text{\sc at}_{\mathrm{top}})
holds.

The Addition Theorem is also called Yuzvinski’s addition formula, since it was first proved by Yuzvinski
[
64
]
for
{\mathbb{Z}}
-actions on compact metrizable groups. This result was extended to
{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}
-actions in by Lind, Schmidt and Ward
[
40
]
(see also
[
53
]
) and further extensions (that now can be seen as particular cases of Li’s result) can be found in
[
43
,
44
]
. These theorems are about measure entropy with respect to the Haar measure, but in all these cases it coincides with the topological entropy.

In §
4.3
, we first give a direct proof of (
at
top
) in the case when
S
is a group.
For this, we use a new approach, that differs from the one adopted in
[
36
,
37
]
as, for example, it does not rely
neither on the Variational Principle nor on Bowen’s definition of entropy in the metrizable case (our approach is close to that used in
[
61
]
, although
these authors treat a different entropy). After that, we deduce the general case of (
at
top
) using two important properties of the Ore colocalization: its “exactness” (i.e., taking the Ore colocalization commutes with restrictions and quotients of an action with respect to a closed invariant subgroup) and the invariance of
h_{\mathrm{top}}
under this construction. These properties together allow us to reduce statements about the additivity of
h_{\mathrm{top}}
for actions of cancellative right amenable monoids to the corresponding questions for actions of amenable groups.

Dual to the notion of topological entropy is that of
algebraic entropy
. Quite surprisingly, the first definition of algebraic entropy for endomorphisms of torsion discrete Abelian groups was given by Adler, Konheim and McAndrew
[
1
]
, in a short final remark of the same paper where the topological entropy was introduced. This algebraic invariant was then studied for endomorphisms of any discrete Abelian group in
[
49
,
63
]
and, more recently, in
[
13
,
16
,
18
,
57
]
(see
[
25
,
26
,
27
]
for the non-Abelian case).

As we did above on the topological side, let us now introduce left actions of a monoid
S
on discrete Abelian groups by endomorphisms. Denote by
{\mathbf{Ab}}
the category of Abelian groups and group homomorphisms, fix a discrete Abelian group
X
and let
\mathrm{End}_{\mathbf{Ab}}(X)
be the endomorphism ring of
X
. A
left
S
-action

S\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 6.0pt$\lambda$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}X

is a monoid homomorphism
\lambda\colon S\to\mathrm{End}_{{\mathbf{Ab}}}(X)
, that is, letting
\lambda_{s}=\lambda(s)\in\mathrm{End}_{\mathbf{Ab}}(X)
for each
s\in S
,

(LA.1)

\lambda_{{1}}=\mathrm{id}_{X}
;

(LA.2)

\lambda_{st}=\lambda_{s}\circ\lambda_{t}
, for all
s,t\in S
.

An endomorphism
f\colon X\to X
uniquely identifies a left action
\lambda
of the additive monoid
{\mathbb{N}}=({\mathbb{N}},+,0)
by setting
\lambda(1)=f
as above. Using this terminology, the algebraic entropy can be seen as an invariant of the left
{\mathbb{N}}
-actions on Abelian groups. As shown in
[
10
]
, the extension given by Ceccherini-Silberstein, Coornaert and Krieger
[
6
]
of the Ornstein-Weiss Lemma allows one to extend the notion of algebraic entropy
h_{\mathrm{alg}}
to left actions
S\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 6.0pt$\lambda$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}X
of cancellative and right amenable monoids
S
on discrete Abelian groups
X
(see §
1.5
).

For any left action
S\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 6.0pt$\lambda$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}X
of a cancellative and right amenable monoid
S
on a discrete Abelian group
X
, there is a canonical left action
G\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 6.0pt$\lambda^{*}$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}X^{*}
of the group of left fractions
G=S^{-1}S
(see §
1.2
) on a new discrete Abelian group
X^{*}
, obtained from
X
and
\lambda
via a suitable direct limit. This important construction, that we call
Ore localization
, is introduced and studied in detail in §
2
. Such localization is built in a two-step process that closely parallels the construction of the Ore colocalization in the topological setting. Indeed, starting with
S\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 6.0pt$\lambda$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}X
, one has first to identify the smallest
S
-invariant subgroup
Y\leq X
, called the
kernel of
\lambda
, for which the action
S\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 6.0pt$\bar{\lambda}$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}\bar{X}=X/Y
induced by
\lambda
is by injective endomorphisms. One then proceeds to build an injective direct system
\mathfrak{X}
of copies of
\bar{X}
, with transition morphisms suitably induced by
\bar{\lambda}
, and to verify that the direct limit
X^{*}
of
\mathfrak{X}
has a natural left
S
-action by automorphisms
S\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 6.0pt$$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}X^{*}
, that uniquely extends to a left
G
-action
G\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 6.0pt$\lambda^{*}$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}X^{*}
, the Ore localization of
S\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 6.0pt$\lambda$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}X
.
Let us remark that the action induced on the quotient over the kernel and the Ore localization satisfy suitable universal properties, that completely characterize them among the left actions by injective endomorphisms and automorphisms, respectively. In particular, both constructions are functorial. Furthermore, they produce exact functors (i.e., they both commute with restrictions and quotients of an action with respect to an invariant subgroup) and they preserve the algebraic entropy, i.e., in the above notation,
h_{\mathrm{alg}}(\lambda)=h_{\mathrm{alg}}(\bar{\lambda})=h_{\mathrm{alg}}(\lambda^{*})
.

Starting from the earliest stages after its introduction, the algebraic entropy was studied in connection with the topological entropy, by means of the Pontryagin duality. To make this idea more precise, let
S
be a monoid,
K
a compact Abelian group and
X={K}^{\wedge}
the (discrete) dual Abelian group of
K
. Denote by
{\mathbf{CompAb}}
the full subcategory of
{\mathbf{CompGr}}
of compact Abelian groups. By the Pontryagin duality we then obtain a ring anti-isomorphism

{(-)}^{\wedge}\colon\mathrm{End}_{{\mathbf{CompAb}}}(K)\to\mathrm{End}_{\mathbf{Ab}}(X),\quad\text{such that}\ \ \varphi\mapsto{\varphi}^{\wedge}.

So, starting with a right
S
-action
K\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 7.0pt$\rho$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowleft\ $}}S
, we get its dual left
S
-action
S\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 6.0pt${\rho}^{\wedge}$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}X
by letting

{\rho}^{\wedge}={(-)}^{\wedge}\circ\rho.

Analogously, starting with a left
S
-action
S\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 6.0pt$\lambda$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}X
on a discrete Abelian group
X
, we get a right
S
-action
K\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 7.0pt${\lambda}^{\wedge}$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowleft\ $}}S
on the compact Abelian group
K={X}^{\wedge}
.
By the Pontryagin duality (see §
1.1
),
\rho^{\wedge\wedge}
is conjugated to
\rho
and
\lambda^{\wedge\wedge}
is conjugated to
\lambda
, so

h_{\mathrm{top}}(\rho^{\wedge\wedge})=h_{\mathrm{top}}(\rho)
and
h_{\mathrm{alg}}(\lambda^{\wedge\wedge})=h_{\mathrm{alg}}(\lambda)
(see §
1.5
and §
1.6
).

For a right
S
-action
K\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 7.0pt$\rho$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowleft\ $}}S
on a compact Abelian group
K
, we say that
the Bridge Theorem holds for
\rho
when the following equality holds:

h_{\mathrm{top}}(\rho)=h_{\mathrm{alg}}({\rho}^{\wedge}).

There are several instances of the Bridge Theorem in the literature; let us list here the most relevant to our situation: the first one was proved by M. D. Weiss
[
63
]
for right
{\mathbb{N}}
-actions on totally disconnected compact Abelian groups (i.e., those for which the dual is a torsion Abelian group), Peters
[
49
]
then verified the Bridge Theorem for right
{\mathbb{Z}}
-actions on compact metrizable Abelian groups. A completely different proof of Peters’ result was given in
[
16
]
, also extending it to right
{\mathbb{N}}
-actions on arbitrary compact Abelian groups, while in
[
24
]
Weiss’ result was extended to semigroup actions. Finally, Peters’ result was extended by Kerr and Li
[
37
]
to actions of countable amenable groups on compact metrizable Abelian groups.
See also
[
50
]
and
[
59
]
for
{\mathbb{Z}}
-actions and amenable group actions, respectively, on general locally compact Abelian groups.
Finally, let us mention that an instance of the Bridge Theorem for actions of sofic groups on compact metrizable Abelian groups has been proved by Liang
[
39
]
.

The second main goal of this paper is to prove the following extension of the known Bridge Theorems:

Bridge Theorem.

Let
S
be a cancellative and right amenable monoid,
K
a compact Abelian group with a right
S
-action
K\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 7.0pt$\rho$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowleft\ $}}S
and
X
a discrete Abelian group with a left
S
-action
S\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 6.0pt$\lambda$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}X
. Then:

(1)

h_{\mathrm{top}}(\rho)=h_{\mathrm{alg}}({\rho}^{\wedge})
;

(2)

h_{\mathrm{alg}}(\lambda)=h_{\mathrm{top}}({\lambda}^{\wedge})
.

The case of the Bridge Theorem when
S
is a group is given in Theorem
A.24
. This result is then extended to the general case in §
5
, by using the invariance of the topological and of the algebraic entropy under Ore colocalization and localization, respectively, and the fact that these two constructions are each other’s dual with respect to the Pontryagin duality, that is, in the above notation,
{(\rho^{*})}^{\wedge}=\lambda^{*}
and
{(\lambda^{*})}^{\wedge}=\rho^{*}
.

The proof of Theorem
A.24
, as well as that of (
at
top
), is heavily based on the uniform approach to entropy
via the entropy of monoid actions on normed monoids developed in
[
12
,
17
]
for
{\mathbb{N}}
-actions and then extended to the general case in
[
58
,
59
]
. This approach covers, beyond the algebraic and the various versions of the topological entropy,
also the measure entropy and many others (see
[
17
]
). It is exposed with more details in §
1.4
.

As an application of the Addition Theorem for
h_{\mathrm{top}}
and of the Bridge Theorem, we show that also
h_{\mathrm{alg}}
satisfies an Addition Theorem for left
S
-actions
S\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 6.0pt$\lambda$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}X
on discrete Abelian groups, so far known only under the hypotheses that either
X
is torsion
[
10
]
or
S
is locally monotileable
[
11
]
:

Algebraic Addition Theorem.

Let
S
be a cancellative and right amenable monoid,
X
an Abelian group,
S\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 6.0pt$\lambda$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}X
a left
S
-action
and
Y
an
S
-invariant subgroup of
X
. Then,

h_{\mathrm{alg}}(\lambda)=h_{\mathrm{alg}}(\lambda_{Y})+h_{\mathrm{alg}}(\lambda_{X/Y}),

[formula]

where
\lambda_{Y}
and
\lambda_{X/Y}
are the obvious left
S
-actions induced by
\lambda
on
Y
and
X/Y
, respectively.

Unlike (
at
top
), (
at
alg
) fails if the group
X
is not Abelian (a simple counterexample involving a
{\mathbb{Z}}
-action on a metabelian group can be found in
[
27
]
).
On the other hand, the first instance of (
at
alg
) for
{\mathbb{N}}
-actions on torsion Abelian groups was given in
[
18
]
, while the general case for
{\mathbb{N}}
-actions on Abelian groups was settled in
[
16
]
. Moreover, (
at
alg
) holds also for
{\mathbb{N}}
-actions on some special classes of non-Abelian groups
[
25
,
26
,
54
]
.

The paper is organized as follows, starting with §
1
, which contains preliminary results and definitions from Pontryagin-van Kampen duality, actions and entropy.

Then, §
2
deals with the algebraic entropy for left actions of cancellative and right amenable monoids on discrete Abelian groups.
A reduction to actions by injective endomorphisms is carried out in §
2.1
.
In §
2.2
we describe the Ore localization of a left action, which is a functorial way to produce an action by automorphisms from any given action, and we establish the invariance of the algebraic entropy under Ore localization. Two different, much more categorical, interpretations of these constructions are then given in §
2.3
.

The case of the topological entropy for right actions of cancellative and right amenable monoids on compact spaces and groups is discussed in §
3
. First, in §
3.2
we obtain a reduction of the computation of the topological entropy to the case of actions by surjective continuous self-maps. Then in §
3.3
we build the Ore colocalization of a right
S
-action on a compact space and we establish the invariance of the topological entropy under this construction. In §
3.4
we give a categorical interpretation of the construction of the Ore colocalization.

The aim of §
4
is to give a proof of (
at
top
) in §
4.3
. Before that, in §
4.1
we study technical properties of the minimum cardinality of finite open covers of compact spaces related to projections and in §
4.2
we discuss special open covers of compact groups, that are used in the proof of
at
top
).

Finally, §
5
contains the proof of the Bridge Theorem and (
at
alg
).

In the appendix, §
A
, we provide a self-contained proof of the Bridge Theorem for amenable group actions, which is a particular case of the general result from
[
59
]
concerning amenable group actions on locally compact Abelian groups.

The readers who prefer to see the proof of (
at
top
) (resp., the Bridge Theorem) only for group actions can go directly to §
4
(resp., §
A
) keeping in mind that the background is recalled in §
1
.

1
Preliminary results and definitions

Here we recall the necessary background. In §
1.1
we include some basic facts regarding the Pontryagin-van Kampen duality.
In §
1.2
we recall that a cancellative and right amenable monoid satisfies the left Ore condition and, therefore, it embeds in its group of left fractions.
In §
1.3
we introduce the formalism of left and right actions and
in §
1.4
the entropy for actions of right amenable cancellative monoids on normed monoids.
In §
1.5
and §
1.6
, we recall the definitions and few basic properties of the algebraic and the topological entropy, respectively.

1.1
The Pontryagin-van Kampen duality for locally compact Abelian groups

In what follows we denote by
{\mathbf{LCA}}
the category of locally compact Abelian groups, with continuous group homomorphisms between them and we identify the categories
{\mathbf{Ab}}
and
{\mathbf{CompAb}}
with the full subcategories of discrete (resp., compact) groups in
{\mathbf{LCA}}
.
We denote by
{\mathbb{T}}={\mathbb{R}}/{\mathbb{Z}}
the torus group, which can be considered both as an object in
{\mathbf{Ab}}
, when taken with its discrete topology, but also as an object in
{\mathbf{CompAb}}
, when taken with the topology inherited from the reals. It will be always clear from the context whether we take
{\mathbb{T}}
as a discrete or as a compact group.

Consider the contravariant functor
{(-)}^{\wedge}\colon{\mathbf{LCA}}\to{\mathbf{LCA}}
that associates to
G\in{\mathbf{LCA}}
the Abelian group of continuous homomorphisms
{G}^{\wedge}=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbf{LCA}}(G,{\mathbb{T}})
, which is a locally compact Abelian group when endowed with the compact-open topology. Furthermore, given a morphism
f\colon G\to H
in
{\mathbf{LCA}}
, we define
{f}^{\wedge}\colon{H}^{\wedge}\to{G}^{\wedge}
as follows:

{f}^{\wedge}\colon\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbf{LCA}}(H,{\mathbb{T}})\to\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbf{LCA}}(G,{\mathbb{T}}),\quad\text{such that}\ \ \chi\mapsto\chi\circ f.

The Pontryagin-van Kampen duality states that for
G\in{\mathbf{LCA}}
there is a natural topological isomorphism
\omega_{G}\colon G\to G^{\wedge\wedge}
, such that
\omega_{G}(x)(\chi)=\chi(x)
for every
x\in G
and
\chi\in{G}^{\wedge}
, i.e.,
(-)^{\wedge}\colon\mathcal{L}\to\mathcal{L}
is an involutive duality of the category
{\mathbf{LCA}}
. Using this duality, we identify
G
with
G^{\wedge\wedge}
whenever needed or convenient. As
{G}^{\wedge}
is compact (resp., discrete) precisely when
G
is discrete (resp., compact), the restrictions of this functor to the categories
{\mathbf{Ab}}
and
{\mathbf{CompAb}}
that, with a slight abuse of notation, we denote by the same symbol, give a duality between the categories
{\mathbf{Ab}}
and
{\mathbf{CompAb}}
.

Given
G\in{\mathbf{LCA}}
, there is an anti-isomorphism between lattices of subobjects (i.e., closed subgroups of
G
and of
{G}^{\wedge}
):

(-)^{\perp}\colon\mathcal{L}(G)\longrightarrow\mathcal{L}({G}^{\wedge}),\quad\text{such that}\quad H\mapsto H^{\perp}=\{\chi\colon G\to{\mathbb{T}}:\chi(H)=0\}.

Furthermore, if
f\colon G\to G^{\prime}
is a morphism in
{\mathbf{LCA}}
and
H\in\mathcal{L}(G)
, then
(f(H))^{\perp}=({f}^{\wedge})^{-1}(H^{\perp})
.
In particular, for
H=G
, this formula gives

\mathrm{Im}(f)^{\perp}=(f(G))^{\perp}=({f}^{\wedge})^{-1}(G^{\perp})=({f}^{\wedge})^{-1}(0)=\mathrm{Ker}({f}^{\wedge}).

See
[
2
]
for these and other properties concerning the Pontryagin-van Kampen duality used in this paper.

1.2
Right amenability and the left Ore condition

Denote by
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{fin}}(A)
the family of all non-empty finite subsets of a set
A
.

A
right Følner net
for a monoid
S
is a net
\{F_{i}\}_{i\in I}
in
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{fin}}(S)
, where
(I,\leq)
is a directed set, such that, for every
s\in S
,

\lim_{i\in I}\frac{|F_{i}s\setminus F_{i}|}{|F_{i}|}=0.

We say that a cancellative monoid
S
is
right amenable
if it admits a right Følner net
[
8
,
45
]
.

Lemma 1.1
.

Let
\{F_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be a right Følner net for a cancellative monoid
S
and take
\{s_{i}\}_{i\in I}\subseteq S
. Then,
\{s_{i}F_{i}\}_{i\in I}
is a right Følner net for
S
.

Proof.

Since
S
is cancellative,
|s_{i}F_{i}|=|F_{i}|
and
|s_{i}F_{i}s\setminus s_{i}F_{i}|=|F_{i}s\setminus F_{i}|
, for all
s\in S
and
i\in I
. Hence, for each
s\in S
,

\lim_{i\in I}\frac{|s_{i}F_{i}s\setminus s_{i}F_{i}|}{|s_{i}F_{i}|}=\lim_{i\in I}\frac{|F_{i}s\setminus F_{i}|}{|F_{i}|}=0,

where the second equality holds as
\{F_{i}\}_{i\in I}
is right Følner. The fact that the limit on the left-hand side of the above formula goes to
0
for all
s\in S
tells us that also
\{s_{i}F_{i}\}_{i\in I}
is right Følner, as desired.
∎

Recall that a cancellative monoid
S
satisfies the
left Ore condition
, or that it is
left Ore
, if:

(LO)

for any pair of elements
s,t\in S
, the intersection
Ss\cap St
is non-empty.

The
right divisibility relation
on a monoid
S
is the following partial preorder:

for
s,s^{\prime}\in S
let
s\leq s^{\prime}
if and only if there exists
t\in S
such that
s^{\prime}=ts
.

Using the right divisibility relation, it is easy to see that the left Ore condition is equivalent to each of the following reformulations:

(LO
′
)

(S,\leq)
is directed, that is, given
s,s^{\prime}\in S
, there is
t\in S
such that
t\geq s
and
t\geq s^{\prime}
;

(LO
′′
)

the subset
Ss\subseteq S
is cofinal in
(S,\leq)
for all
s\in S
, that is, given
s,s^{\prime}\in S
there exists
t\in S
such that
ts\geq s^{\prime}
.

In the following lemma we collect a couple of less obvious consequences of the left Ore condition:

Lemma 1.2
.

Let
S
be a left Ore monoid and
s,s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}\in S
. Then:

(1)

there exist
t_{1},\ldots,t_{n},t\in S
such that
t_{j}s_{k}=ts
for every
j=1,\ldots,n
;

(2)

there exist
t_{1},\ldots,t_{n},t\in S
such that
ts_{j}=t_{j}s
for every
j=1,\ldots,n
.

Proof.

(1) By (LO), there exist
l_{1},k_{1}\in S
such that
l_{1}s_{1}=k_{1}s
. Similarly, one can find
l_{2},k_{2}\in S
such that
l_{2}s_{2}=k_{2}k_{1}s
; in particular,
k_{2}l_{1}s_{1}=k_{2}k_{1}s
. Proceeding by induction, we find
l_{1},\ldots,l_{n}\in S
and
k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}\in S
such that
l_{j}s_{j}=k_{j}k_{j-1}\ldots k_{1}s
for every
j=1,\ldots,n
. One concludes by letting
t=k_{n}\ldots k_{1}
, and
t_{j}=k_{n}\ldots k_{j+1}l_{j}
.

(2) We first show that there exist
v_{1},\ldots,v_{n},t^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,t^{\prime}_{n}\in S
such that

(t^{\prime}_{j}\ldots t^{\prime}_{1})s_{j}=v_{j}s,\quad\text{for all $j=1,\ldots,n$}.

(1.1)

Indeed, by (LO), there exist
t_{1}^{\prime},v_{1}\in S
such that
t_{1}^{\prime}s_{1}=v_{1}s
. Then there exist
t^{\prime}_{2},v_{2}\in S
such that
t^{\prime}_{2}t^{\prime}_{1}s_{2}=v_{2}s
. Proceeding in this way by induction
we find the last pair
v_{n},t^{\prime}_{n}\in S
such that
(t^{\prime}_{n}\ldots t^{\prime}_{1})s_{n}=v_{n}s
.
Put
t=t^{\prime}_{n}\ldots t^{\prime}_{1}
and

u_{j}=\begin{cases}t_{n}^{\prime}\ldots t^{\prime}_{j+1}&\text{if $j<n$;}\\
1&\text{if $n=1$.}\end{cases}

Then,
ts_{j}=u_{j}(t^{\prime}_{j}\ldots t^{\prime}_{1})s_{j}=u_{j}v_{j}s
, in view of (
1.1
). Letting
t_{j}=u_{j}v_{j}
, we deduce that
ts_{j}=t_{j}s
, for every
j=1,\ldots,n
.
∎

If
S
is a cancellative and left Ore monoid, it can be embedded in a group
G=S^{-1}S
that we call
group of left fractions
of
S
(see
[
48
, Proposition 1.26]
). Clearly
G
is generated as a group by
S
.
Furthermore, it is well-known (see
[
48
, Proposition 1.23]
) that a cancellative and right amenable monoid
S
is automatically left Ore. In the following lemma (which is
[
10
, Lemma 2.11]
) we recall the useful fact that the right amenability of
S
implies that its group of left fractions
G
is amenable:

Lemma 1.3
.

Let
G
be a group and
S
a submonoid of
G
that generates
G
as a group.

(1)

If
S
is right amenable (and, necessarily, cancellative), then
G
is amenable.

(2)

If
\{F_{i}\}_{i\in I}
is a right Følner net for
S
, then it is also a right Følner net for
G
.

Next we endow
G
with the preorder relation
\leq_{S}
, called the
S
-preorder
on
G
, that makes it into a directed set:

g_{1}\leq_{S}g_{2}
if and only if
g_{2}^{-1}g_{1}\in S
, for all
g_{1},g_{2}\in G
.

In other words, for
g_{1},g_{2}\in G
,
g_{1}\leq_{S}g_{2}
if and only if there exists
s\in S
such that
g_{1}=g_{2}s
.

Lemma 1.4
.

Let
S
be a cancellative left Ore monoid and
G=S^{-1}S
its group of left fractions. Then, the preordered set
(G,\leq_{S})
is directed.

Proof.

Consider two elements
g_{1},g_{2}\in G
and write them as
g_{1}=s_{1}^{-1}t_{1}
and
g_{2}=s_{2}^{-1}t_{2}
for suitable
s_{1},s_{2},t_{1},t_{2}\in S
(this is always possible because
G=S^{-1}S
is the group of left fractions of
S
). Using the left Ore condition, choose
u_{1}
and
u_{2}\in S
such that
u_{1}s_{1}=u_{2}s_{2}
and let
g=s_{1}^{-1}u_{1}^{-1}=s_{2}^{-1}u_{2}^{-1}\in G
. Then,

g^{-1}g_{1}=(u_{1}s_{1})(s_{1}^{-1}t_{1})=u_{1}t_{1}\in S\quad\text{and}\quad g^{-1}g_{2}=(u_{2}s_{2})(s_{2}^{-1}t_{2})=u_{2}t_{2}\in S.

Hence, we both have that
g_{1}\leq_{S}g
and
g_{2}\leq_{S}g
, showing that
(G,\leq_{S})
is directed.
∎

The main reason to use two different symbols for the right divisibility relation on
S
and for the
S
-preorder on
G
is that these two preorders do not coincide on
S
: actually, they are opposite to each other, that is,
s_{1}\leq s_{2}
in
S
if and only if
s_{2}\leq_{S}s_{1}
in
G
; they coincide precisely when
S
is a group
(that is, the two preorders coincide on
S
if and only if they are both trivial, if and only if
S=G
). In the following sections both preordered sets will turn out to be very important.

1.3
Left and right representations

Given a monoid
S
and a category
{\mathcal{C}}
, we denote by
\mathrm{LRep}(S,{\mathcal{C}})
and
\mathrm{RRep}(S,{\mathcal{C}})
the categories of left and right representations in
{\mathcal{C}}
, respectively.

The objects of
\mathrm{LRep}(S,{\mathcal{C}})
are the left
S
-actions, that is, the pairs
(X,\lambda)
, denoted by
S\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 6.0pt$\lambda$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}X
in the sequel, where
X
is an object of
{\mathcal{C}}
and
\lambda\colon S\to\mathrm{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(X)
is a monoid homomorphism. A morphism
f\colon(X,\lambda)\to(X^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime})
in
\mathrm{LRep}(S,{\mathcal{C}})
is a morphism
f\colon X\to X^{\prime}
in
{\mathcal{C}}
that is
S
-equivariant
, i.e., such that the following diagram commutes, for each
s\in S
:

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{\lambda_{s}}
\textstyle{X^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda^{\prime}_{s}}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{X^{\prime}.}

If there exists an
S
-equivariant isomorphism between
X
and
X^{\prime}
, its inverse is automatically
S
-equivariant, and we say that the two left
S
-actions
S\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 6.0pt$\lambda$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}X
and
S\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 6.0pt$\lambda^{\prime}$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}X^{\prime}
are
isomorphic
or
conjugated
. In other words, two left
S
-actions are conjugated if and only if they are isomorphic in the category
\mathrm{LRep}(S,{\mathcal{C}})
.

The monomorphisms in
\mathrm{LRep}(S,{\mathcal{C}})
are exactly the
S
-equivariant morphisms that are monic in
{\mathcal{C}}
. In particular, the subobjects of a left
S
-action
S\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 6.0pt$\lambda$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}X
are all of the form
\iota\colon Y\to X
, where
\iota
is a monomorphism in
{\mathcal{C}}
, and
Y
is
S
-invariant
in
X
, that is, for each
s\in S
, the composition
\lambda_{s}\circ\iota
factors through
\iota
, i.e., there exists a (necessarily unique) morphism
(\lambda_{Y})_{s}\colon Y\to Y
such that
\lambda_{s}\circ\iota=\iota\circ(\lambda_{Y})_{s}
. In this case,
Y
is endowed with the unique possible action that makes
\iota
into an
S
-equivariant morphism:

S\overset{\lambda_{Y}}{\curvearrowright}Y\quad\text{with}\quad\lambda_{Y}(s)=(\lambda_{Y})_{s},\text{ for all $s\in S$}.

Objects, morphisms, conjugations and subobjects in the category
\mathrm{RRep}(S,{\mathcal{C}})
are defined similarly.

In the sequel, the category of left representations
\mathrm{LRep}(S,{\mathcal{C}})
will only appear with
{\mathcal{C}}={\mathbf{Ab}}
and with
{\mathcal{C}}
the category of normed monoids introduced in §
1.4
below. On the other hand,
\mathrm{RRep}(S,{\mathcal{C}})
will appear with
{\mathcal{C}}={\mathbf{CompSp}}
,
{\mathcal{C}}={\mathbf{CompGr}}
and
{\mathcal{C}}={\mathbf{CompAb}}
.

Remark 1.5
.

When
S
is a cancellative and right amenable monoid, with
G=S^{-1}S
its group of left fractions, one can consider
\mathrm{LRep}(G,{\mathbf{Ab}})
simply as a full subcategory of
\mathrm{LRep}(S,{\mathbf{Ab}})
, by taking the restriction of a
G
-action to an
S
-action. In §
2
we build, for each
S\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 6.0pt$\lambda$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}X\in\mathrm{LRep}(S,{\mathbf{Ab}})
, a canonical
G
-action
G\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 6.0pt$\lambda^{*}$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}X^{*}\in\mathrm{LRep}(G,{\mathbf{Ab}})
, functorially associated with
\lambda
, called the Ore localization of
\lambda
(see Definition
2.7
). For a deeper analysis, using a heavier impact of categorical algebra, see §
2.3
.

Similarly,
\mathrm{RRep}(G,{\mathbf{CompSp}})
(resp.,
\mathrm{RRep}(G,{\mathbf{CompGr}})
,
\mathrm{RRep}(G,{\mathbf{CompAb}})
) can be viewed as a full subcategory of
\mathrm{RRep}(S,{\mathbf{CompSp}})
(resp.,
\mathrm{RRep}(S,{\mathbf{CompGr}})
,
\mathrm{RRep}(S,{\mathbf{CompAb}})
). In §
3
we build the Ore colocalization of a given right
S
-action
K\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 7.0pt$\rho$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowleft\ $}}S
in
\mathrm{RRep}(S,{\mathbf{CompSp}})
. This is a canonical right
G
-action
K^{*}\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 7.0pt$\rho^{*}$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowleft\ $}}G
in
\mathrm{RRep}(G,{\mathbf{CompSp}})
, functorially associated with
\rho
(see Definition
3.13
). As for the Ore localization, we give a categorical interpretation of the Ore colocalization in §
3.4
.

1.4
Entropy of actions on normed monoids

Here we recall the notion of entropy for actions of cancellative and right amenable monoids on normed monoids, and the notions of asymptotic equivalence, introduced in
[
58
]
(see also
[
59
]
), and that of weak asymptotic equivalence, that conveniently weaken the more rigid condition of being conjugated. Let
S
be a fixed cancellative and right amenable monoid for the rest of this section.

1.4.1
The category of normed monoids

A
normed monoid
is a pair
(M,v)
, where
(M,+,0)
is a commutative monoid and
v\colon M\to{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}
is a function.

Given a normed monoid
(M,v)
, we say that the norm
v
is:

–

monotone
provided
v(x)\leq v(x+y)
, for all
x
,
y\in M
;

–

subadditive
provided
v(x+y)\leq v(x)+v(y)
, for all
x
,
y\in M
.

A
homomorphism of normed monoids

f\colon(M_{1},v_{1})\to(M_{2},v_{2})
is a monoid homomorphism
f\colon M_{1}\to M_{2}
such that
v_{2}(f(m))\leq v_{1}(m)
for all
m\in M_{1}
. Furthermore,
f
is an
isomorphism
if it is a monoid isomorphism and
v_{2}(f(m))=v_{1}(m)
for all
m\in M_{1}
. We denote by
\mathfrak{M}
the category of normed monoids defined in this way.

The algebraic and the topological entropy are based on the following normed monoids (see §
1.5
and §
1.6
).

Example 1.6
.

Let
X
be a discrete Abelian group.

(1)

Denote by
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{fin}}^{0}(X)
the family of all finite subsets of
X
that contain
0
. The pair
(\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{fin}}^{0}(X),+)
is a commutative monoid, with norm
v_{\mathfrak{F}}
defined by
v_{\mathfrak{F}}(H)=\log|H|
, for all
H\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{fin}}^{0}(X)
. The norm
v_{\mathfrak{F}}
is both monotone and subadditive.

(2)

The submonoid
\mathfrak{F}(X)
of
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{fin}}^{0}(X)
, consisting of all finite symmetric subsets of
X
that contain
0
, will also be used in the sequel;
\mathfrak{F}(X)
is cofinal in
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{fin}}^{0}(X)
with respect to the inclusion
\subseteq
.

(3)

Another submonoid of
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{fin}}^{0}(X)
is
\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{fin}}(X)
consisting of all finite subgroups of
X
; clearly,
\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{fin}}(X)\subseteq\mathfrak{F}(X)
and
\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{fin}}(X)
is cofinal in
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{fin}}^{0}(X)
(if and only if it is cofinal in
\mathfrak{F}(X)
) precisely when
X
is torsion.

Example 1.7
.

Let
K
be a compact space. Denote by
\mathrm{cov}(K)
the family of all open covers of
K
and recall that for
\mathcal{U},\mathcal{V}\in\mathrm{cov}(K)
,

\mathcal{U}\vee\mathcal{V}=\{U\cap V:U\in{\cal U},V\in{\cal V}\}\in\mathrm{cov}(K)
. For a subset
B
of
K
and
\mathcal{U}\in\mathrm{cov}(K)
, let

N_{B}(\mathcal{U})=\min\{n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{+}\colon\mathcal{U}\ \text{admits a subcover of $B$ of size $n$}\};

when
B=K
we simply write
N(\mathcal{U})
instead of
N_{K}(\mathcal{U})
.

Then
(\mathrm{cov}(K),\vee)
is a commutative monoid with a monotone and subadditive norm
v_{\mathrm{cov}}
given by
v_{\mathrm{cov}}(\mathcal{U})=\log N(\mathcal{U})
, for all
\mathcal{U}\in\mathrm{cov}(K)
.

Example 1.8
.

Now assume that
K
is a compact group and
\mu
is the Haar measure on
K
(so that
\mu(K)=1
).

(1)

Let
{\mathfrak{U}}(K)
be the family of all symmetric compact neighborhoods of
1
in
K
. Then the pair
({\mathfrak{U}}(K),\cap)
is a commutative monoid, with norm
v_{\mathfrak{U}}
defined by
v_{\mathfrak{U}}(U)=-\log\mu(U)
, for each
U\in{\mathfrak{U}}(K)
. Clearly,
v_{\mathfrak{U}}
is monotone, but not subadditive in general.

(2)

Similarly, the larger family of
all
compact neighborhoods of
1
in
K
with
\cap
as operation and
v_{\mathfrak{U}}
as above, is a normed monoid.
Then
\mathfrak{U}(K)
is a submonoid of this monoid, cofinal with respect to
\supseteq
.
Our preference for
\mathfrak{U}(K)
is motivated by its application in §
A
, where the symmetry of its elements is needed.

(3)

One can also consider the submonoid
\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{o}}(K)
of
\mathfrak{U}(K)
consisting of all open subgroups of
K
.
When
U\in\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{o}}(K)
, clearly
U
has finite index
[K:U]
and
\mu(U)=1/[K:U]
, so
v_{\mathfrak{U}}(U)=\log[K:U]
. In particular, when restricted to
\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{o}}(K)
, the norm
v_{\mathfrak{U}}
is also subadditive.

(4)

With
X={K}^{\wedge}
, the map
\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{fin}}(X)\to\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{o}}(K)
defined by
F\mapsto F^{\perp}
is a monoid isomorphism (by the Pontryagin duality – see §
1.1
) and
\log|F|=\log[K:F^{\perp}]
for every
F\in\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{fin}}(X)
; that is, we have an isomorphism of normed monoids
\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{fin}}(X)\cong\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{o}}(K)
.

(5)

By identifying each
U\in\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{o}}(K)
with
{\cal U}_{K}[U]=\{xU:x\in K\}\in\mathrm{cov}(K)
,
\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{o}}(K)
can also be considered as a normed submonoid of
\mathrm{cov}(K)
, since, for every
U,V\in\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{o}}(K)
,
{\cal U}_{K}[U\cap V]={\cal U}_{K}[U]\vee{\cal U}_{K}[V]
and
[K:U]=N({\cal U}_{K}(U))
.

(6)

The compact group
K
is profinite precisely when
\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{o}}(K)
is cofinal in
\mathfrak{U}(K)
or, equivalently, when
\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{o}}(K)
is cofinal in
\mathrm{cov}(K)
(see Lemma
4.7
(1)).

1.4.2
Asymptotic domination

Let
S
be a cancellative and right amenable monoid. A left
S
-action
S\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 6.0pt$\alpha$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}M
on a normed monoid
M
is a monoid homomorphism
\alpha\colon S\to\mathrm{End}_{\mathfrak{M}}(M)
(where
\mathrm{End}_{\mathfrak{M}}(M)
is the monoid of all endomorphisms of normed monoids
M\to M
). For
x\in M
and
F=\{f_{1},\dots,f_{k}\}\subseteq S
, we let

T_{F}(\alpha,x)=\alpha_{f_{1}}(x)+\ldots+\alpha_{f_{k}}(x).

Recall from §
1.3
that two left
S
-actions
S\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 6.0pt$\alpha_{1}$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}M_{1}
and
S\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 6.0pt$\alpha_{1}$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}M_{1}
on the two normed monoids
(M_{1},v_{1})
and
(M_{2},v_{2})
are conjugated if there exists an
S
-equivariant isomorphism of normed monoids
f\colon M_{1}\to M_{2}
. Now we introduce three notions of “equivalence”, all weaker than conjugation, between actions on normed monoids:

Definition 1.9
.

Let
S\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 6.0pt$\alpha_{1}$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}M_{1}
and
S\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 6.0pt$\alpha_{2}$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}M_{2}
be two left
S
-actions, where
M_{j}=(M_{j},v_{j})
is a normed monoid for
j=1,2
. We say that:

(i)

\alpha_{2}

dominates

\alpha_{1}
if, for each
x\in M_{1}
, there exists
y\in M_{2}
such that, for each
F\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{fin}}(S)
,

v_{1}(T_{F}(\alpha_{1},x))\leq v_{2}(T_{F}(\alpha_{2},y));

(ii)

\alpha_{2}

asymptotically dominates

\alpha_{1}
if, for each
x\in M_{1}
, there are two sequences
\{y_{n}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
in
M_{2}
and
\{\varepsilon_{n}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
in
{\mathbb{R}}
such that
\lim_{n\to\infty}\varepsilon_{n}=0
and, for each
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
F\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{fin}}(S)
,

\frac{v_{1}(T_{F}(\alpha_{1},x))}{|F|}\leq\frac{v_{2}(T_{F}(\alpha_{2},y_{n}))}{|F|}+\varepsilon_{n};

(iii)

\alpha_{2}

weakly asymptotically dominates

\alpha_{1}
if, for every right Følner net
{\mathfrak{s}}=\{F_{i}\}_{i\in I}
for
S
and for every
x\in M_{1}
, there exist a sequence
\{y_{n}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
in
M_{2}
and a sequence
\{f_{n}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
of functions
f_{n}\colon{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}\to{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}
such that:

(iii
1
)

\{f_{n}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
converges uniformly to
\mathrm{id}_{{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}}\colon{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}\to{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}
on every bounded interval
[0,C]
, that is, for every
\varepsilon>0
there exists
\bar{n}\in{\mathbb{N}}
, such that
|f_{n}(r)-r|<\varepsilon
for every
n\geq\bar{n}
in
{\mathbb{N}}
and for every
r\in[0,C]
;

(iii
2
)

there exists
j\in I
such that, for every
i\geq j
in
I
and for every
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,

\frac{v_{1}(T_{F_{i}}(\alpha_{1},x))}{|F_{i}|}\leq f_{n}\left(\frac{v_{2}(T_{F_{i}}(\alpha_{2},y_{n}))}{|F_{i}|}\right);

(iv)

\alpha_{1}
is
equivalent
(resp.,
asymptotically equivalent
,
weakly asymptotically equivalent
) to
\alpha_{2}
if the two actions dominate (resp., asymptotically dominate, weakly asymptotically dominate) each other.

Clearly, conjugated actions are equivalent, and equivalent actions are asymptotically equivalent. Here are two examples when domination arises in a rather natural way.

Example 1.10
.

Let
S\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 6.0pt$\alpha_{1}$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}M_{1}
and
S\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 6.0pt$\alpha_{2}$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}M_{2}
be two left
S
-actions on normed monoids, and assume that
f\colon M_{1}\to M_{2}
is an
S
-equivariant morphism in
\mathfrak{M}
.

(1)

If
f
is an embedding (i.e.,
f
is injective and
f\colon M_{1}\to f(M_{1})
is an isomorphism in
\mathfrak{M}
), then
\alpha_{2}

dominates

\alpha_{1}
(to check it, for
x\in M_{1}
put
y=f(x))
.

(2)

If
f
is surjective, then
\alpha_{1}

dominates

\alpha_{2}
(to check it, for
x\in M_{2}
pick
y\in M_{1}
such that
x=f(y))
.

The following is another natural example of an equivalence.

Example 1.11
.

Let
S\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 6.0pt$\alpha_{2}$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}M_{2}
be a left
S
-action on a normed monoid
M_{2}=(M_{2},v_{2})
,
M_{1}
an
S
-invariant submonoid of
M_{2}
, endowed with the norm
(v_{2})_{\restriction M_{1}}
, and
S\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 6.0pt$\alpha_{1}$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}M_{1}
the restriction of the action
\alpha_{2}
to
M_{1}
. According to Example
1.10
(1),
\alpha_{2}
dominates
\alpha_{1}
.

Now assume that
M_{2}
is preordered, with the norm
v_{2}
monotone with respect to the preorder (i.e., if
x\leq y
in
M_{2}
, then
v_{2}(x)\leq v_{2}(y)
). If
M_{1}
is cofinal in
M_{2}
, then also
\alpha_{1}
dominates
\alpha_{2}
, and so we can conclude that
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}
are equivalent. This permits to restrict from a normed monoid to a convenient cofinal
S
-invariant submonoid.

Lemma 1.12
.

Let
S\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 6.0pt$\alpha_{1}$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}M_{1}
and
S\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 6.0pt$\alpha_{2}$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}M_{2}
be two left
S
-actions on normed monoids. If
\alpha_{2}
asymptotically dominates
\alpha_{1}
, then
\alpha_{2}
weakly asymptotically dominates
\alpha_{1}
.

Proof.

Let
x\in M_{1}
. There exist two sequences
\{y_{n}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
in
M_{2}
and
\{\varepsilon_{n}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
in
{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}
such that
\lim_{n\to\infty}\varepsilon_{n}=0
and, for every
F\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{fin}}(S)
,

v_{1}(T_{F}(\alpha_{1},x))\leq v_{2}(T_{F}(\alpha_{2},y_{n}))+\varepsilon_{n}|F|.

For every
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, define
f_{n}\colon{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}\to{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}
by
f_{n}(r)=r+\varepsilon_{n}
for every
r\in{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}
.
Then
f_{n}
converges uniformly to
\mathrm{id}_{{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}}
on all of
{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}
.
If
{\mathfrak{s}}=\{F_{i}\}_{i\in I}
is a right Følner net for
S
, then for every
i\in I
,

\frac{v_{1}(T_{F_{i}}(\alpha_{1},x))}{|F_{i}|}\leq\frac{v_{2}(T_{F_{i}}(\alpha_{2},y_{n}))+\varepsilon_{n}|F_{i}|}{|F_{i}|}=f_{n}\left(\frac{v_{2}(T_{F_{i}}(\alpha_{2},y_{n}))}{|F_{i}|}\right).\qed

1.4.3
Entropy and its properties

Definition 1.13

(
[
17
,
59
]
)
.

Let
M=(M,v)
be a normed monoid,
S\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 6.0pt$\alpha$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}M
a left
S
-action and fix a right Følner net
{\mathfrak{s}}=\{F_{i}\}_{i\in I}
for
S
. The
{\mathfrak{s}}
-entropy
of
\alpha
at a given
m\in M
is

H(\alpha,{\mathfrak{s}},m)=\limsup_{i\in I}\frac{v(T_{F_{i}}(\alpha,m))}{|F_{i}|}.

The
{\mathfrak{s}}
-entropy
of
\alpha
is
h(\alpha,{\mathfrak{s}})=\sup_{m\in M}H(\alpha,{\mathfrak{s}},m)
.

If the norm
v
is also subadditive, then our definition of entropy can be sensibly improved:

Lemma 1.14
.

Let
M=(M,v)
be a normed monoid with
v
monotone and subadditive, and let
S\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 6.0pt$\alpha$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}M
be a left
S
-action. For each
m\in M
and each right Følner net
{\mathfrak{s}}=\{F_{i}\}_{i\in I}
for
S
, the limit superior defining
H(\alpha,{\mathfrak{s}},m)
is a limit, which does not depend on the choice of
{\mathfrak{s}}
.

Proof.

For
m\in M
, consider the function
f_{m}\colon\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{fin}}(S)\to{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}
such that
f_{m}(F)=v(T_{F}(\alpha,m))
. Each
f_{m}
has the following properties, which allow one to conclude by
[
6
, Corollary 1.2]
:

–

subadditivity
, that is, given
F,F^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{fin}}(S)
, we have:

\displaystyle f_{m}(F\cup F^{\prime})

\displaystyle=v(T_{F\cup F^{\prime}}(\alpha,m))

\displaystyle=v(T_{F}(\alpha,m)+T_{F^{\prime}\setminus F}(\alpha,m))

\displaystyle\leq v(T_{F}(\alpha,m))+v(T_{F^{\prime}\setminus F}(\alpha,m))

by subadditivity of
v
;

\displaystyle\leq v(T_{F}(\alpha,m))+v(T_{F^{\prime}}(\alpha,m))=f_{m}(F)+f_{m}(F^{\prime})

by monotonicity of
v
.

–

left subinvariance
, that is, given
F\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{fin}}(S)
and
s\in S
,

f_{m}(sF)=v(T_{sF}(\alpha,m))=v(\alpha_{s}(T_{F}(\alpha,m)))\overset{(*)}{\leq}v(T_{F}(\alpha,m))=f_{m}(F),

where
(*)
follows since
\alpha_{s}
is a homomorphism of normed monoids.
∎

For normed monoids
M_{1}=(M_{1},v_{1})
,
M_{2}=(M_{2},v_{2})
and left
S
-actions
S\overset{\alpha_{1}}{\curvearrowright}M_{1}
,
S\overset{\alpha_{2}}{\curvearrowright}M_{2}
, define the coproduct
M_{1}\oplus M_{2}
with the norm
v_{1}\oplus v_{2}
such that
(v_{1}\oplus v_{2})(x,y)=v_{1}(x)+v_{2}(y)
for every
(x,y)\in M_{1}\oplus M_{2}
, and the left
S
-action

S\hskip 5.0pt\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{6.02773pt}{\hskip-3.0pt$\alpha_{1}\oplus\alpha_{2}$}}}{\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}\hskip 6.0ptM_{1}\oplus M_{2},\quad\text{such that, for every $s\in S$, $(\alpha_{1}\oplus\alpha_{2})_{s}(x,y)=((\alpha_{1})_{s}(x),(\alpha_{2})_{s}(y))$ for every $(x,y)\in M_{1}\oplus M_{2}$.}

If both
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
are monotone (resp., subadditive), then so is
v_{1}\oplus v_{2}
.
In the proof of (
at
top
) we use the following weak Addition Theorem (we omit its straightforward proof).

Proposition 1.15
.

In the above notation, if
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
are both monotone and subadditive, then

h(\alpha_{1}\oplus\alpha_{2},{\mathfrak{s}})=h(\alpha_{1},{\mathfrak{s}})+h(\alpha_{2},{\mathfrak{s}})

for any right Følner net
{\mathfrak{s}}
for
S
.

The key to the proof of both the Topological Addition Theorem and the Bridge Theorem is the following:

Proposition 1.16
.

Let
M_{1}=(M_{1},v_{1})
,
M_{2}=(M_{2},v_{2})
be normed monoids,
S\overset{\alpha_{1}}{\curvearrowright}M_{1}
,
S\overset{\alpha_{2}}{\curvearrowright}M_{2}
left
S
-actions, and
{\mathfrak{s}}=\{F_{i}\}_{i\in I}
a right Følner net for
S
. If
S\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 6.0pt$\alpha_{2}$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}M_{2}
weakly asymptotically dominates
S\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 6.0pt$\alpha_{1}$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}M_{1}
, then
h(\alpha_{1},{\mathfrak{s}})\leq h(\alpha_{2},{\mathfrak{s}})
.
In particular,
h(\alpha_{1},{\mathfrak{s}})=h(\alpha_{2},{\mathfrak{s}})
whenever
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}
are weakly asymptotically equivalent.

Proof.

If
h(\alpha_{2},{\mathfrak{s}})=\infty
, we are done, so assume
h(\alpha_{2},{\mathfrak{s}})<\infty
.
We verify that for every
x\in M_{1}
,
H(\alpha_{1},{\mathfrak{s}},x)\leq h(\alpha_{2},{\mathfrak{s}})
. Let

\{y_{n}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
,
\{f_{n}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
and
j\in I
be as in the definition. Then, for every
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and
j\leq i\in I

\frac{v_{1}(T_{F_{i}}(\alpha_{1},x))}{|F_{i}|}\leq f_{n}\left(\frac{v_{2}(T_{F_{i}}(\alpha_{2},y_{n}))}{|F_{i}|}\right)

(1.2)

Fix an arbitrary
\varepsilon>0
and put
C=h(\alpha_{2},{\mathfrak{s}})+1
. There exists
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that

|f_{n}(r)-r|<\varepsilon\ \mbox{ for every }n\geq m\ \mbox{ and }r\in[0,C].

(1.3)

Since

\lim_{j\leq i\in I}\frac{v_{2}(T_{F_{i}}(\alpha_{2},y_{m}))}{|F_{i}|}=H(\alpha_{2},{\mathfrak{s}},y_{m})\leq h(\alpha_{2},{\mathfrak{s}})<h(\alpha_{2},{\mathfrak{s}})+1=C,

there exists
j_{m}\in I
such that

\frac{v_{2}(T_{F_{i}}(\alpha_{2},y_{m}))}{|F_{i}|}\leq C\ \mbox{ for every }\ i\geq j_{m}.

(1.4)

Now pick a
j_{0}
such that
j_{0}\geq j
and
j_{0}\geq j_{m}
. Then, combining (
1.2
), (
1.3
) and (
1.4
) we deduce that

\frac{v_{1}(T_{F_{i}}(\alpha_{1},x))}{|F_{i}|}\leq\frac{v_{2}(T_{F_{i}}(\alpha_{2},y_{m}))}{|F_{i}|}+\varepsilon\ \mbox{ for every }\ i\geq j_{0}.

After taking limits, this gives

H(\alpha_{1},{\mathfrak{s}},x)\leq H(\alpha_{2},{\mathfrak{s}},y_{m})+\varepsilon\leq h(\alpha_{2},{\mathfrak{s}})+\varepsilon.

Since
\varepsilon>0
was chosen arbitrarily, we conclude that
H(\alpha_{1},{\mathfrak{s}},x)\leq h(\alpha_{2},{\mathfrak{s}})
.
As this holds for each
x\in M_{1}
, we get the desired inequality
h(\alpha_{1},{\mathfrak{s}})\leq h(\alpha_{2},{\mathfrak{s}})
. The second assertion obviously follows from the first one.
∎

Example
1.10
and the above proposition give:

Corollary 1.17
.

With
S\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 6.0pt$\alpha_{1}$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}M_{1}
and
S\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 6.0pt$\alpha_{2}$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}M_{2}
as above, and
f\colon M_{1}\to M_{2}
an
S
-equivariant morphism in
\mathfrak{M}
, for
{\mathfrak{s}}
a right Følner net for
S
,

(1)

h(\alpha_{1},{\mathfrak{s}})\leq h(\alpha_{2},{\mathfrak{s}})
, if
f
is an embedding;

(2)

h(\alpha_{1},{\mathfrak{s}})\geq h(\alpha_{2},{\mathfrak{s}})
, if
f
is surjective.

1.5
Background on the algebraic entropy

In this subsection we recall the definition and some of the basic properties of the algebraic entropy
h_{\mathrm{alg}}(\lambda)
of the left action
S\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 6.0pt$\lambda$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}X
of the cancellative and right amenable monoid
S
on a discrete Abelian group
X
.

Given
F\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{fin}}(S)
and
E\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{fin}}^{0}(X)
, we let

T_{F\,}(\lambda,E)=\sum_{s\in F}\lambda_{s}(E).

Definition 1.18
.

For
E\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{fin}}^{0}(X)
, the
algebraic entropy of
\lambda
with respect to
E
is

H_{\mathrm{alg}}(\lambda,E)=\lim_{i\in I}\frac{\log|T_{F_{i}}(\lambda,E)|}{|F_{i}|}.

(1.5)

The
algebraic entropy of
\lambda
is
h_{\mathrm{alg}}(\lambda)=\sup\{H_{\mathrm{alg}}(\lambda,E):E\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{fin}}^{0}(X)\}.

Here is an alternative way to arrive at this notion which allows us to easily obtain many of the properties of
h_{\mathrm{alg}}
.

Example 1.19
.

Let
X
be a discrete Abelian group.

(1)

The assignment
X\mapsto\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{fin}}^{0}(X)
from Example
1.6
can be completed to a covariant functor
\mathcal{P}\colon{\mathbf{Ab}}\to\mathfrak{M}
by letting
\mathcal{P}(f)(E)=f(E)
for any morphism
f\colon X\to Y
in
{\mathbf{Ab}}
and
E\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{fin}}^{0}(X)
. If
j\colon Y\to X
is a subgroup embedding in
{\mathbf{Ab}}
,
then the map
\mathcal{P}(j)\colon\mathcal{P}(Y)\to\mathcal{P}(X)
is an embedding in
\mathfrak{M}
, while the quotient map
q\colon X\to X/Y
gives rise to a surjective morphism
\mathcal{P}(q)\colon\mathcal{P}(X)\to\mathcal{P}(X/Y)
in
\mathfrak{M}
.

(2)

For a left
S
-action
S\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 6.0pt$\lambda$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}X
, the functor
\mathcal{P}
induces a left
S
-action

S\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 6.0pt$\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{fin}}^{0}(X),\quad\text{ with }\ (\lambda_{\mathcal{P}})_{s}(E)=\lambda_{s}(E)\ \text{for every $s\in S$ and $E\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{fin}}^{0}(X)$}.

For any
F\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{fin}}(S)
and
E\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{fin}}^{0}(X)
,

T_{F}(\lambda_{\mathcal{P}},E)=\sum_{s\in F}\lambda_{s}(E)=T_{F}(\lambda,E).

So, just by definition, for a given right Følner net
{\mathfrak{s}}
for
S
,
H_{\mathrm{alg}}(\lambda,E)=H(\lambda_{\mathcal{P}},{\mathfrak{s}},E)
and
h_{\mathrm{alg}}(\lambda)=h(\lambda_{\mathcal{P}},{\mathfrak{s}})
.

Hence, the existence of the limit in (
1.5
) and its independence on the choice of
{\mathfrak{s}}
follow from Lemma
1.14
.
This freedom in the choice of the right Følner net will be fundamental in the proofs of the following sections.

(3)

The submonoid
\mathfrak{F}(X)
of
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{fin}}^{0}(X)
is
S
-invariant, so we can consider the restriction
S\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 6.0pt$\lambda_{\mathfrak{F}}$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}\mathfrak{F}(X)
of
\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}
to
\mathfrak{F}(X)
.
By Example
1.11
,
\lambda_{\mathfrak{F}}
and
\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}
are equivalent, since
\mathfrak{F}(X)
is cofinal in
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{fin}}^{0}(X)
.
Hence, for any right Følner net
{\mathfrak{s}}
for
S
,

h_{\mathrm{alg}}(\lambda)=h(\lambda_{\mathcal{P}},{\mathfrak{s}})=h(\lambda_{\mathfrak{F}},{\mathfrak{s}})

by Proposition
1.16
.
Therefore, for the computation of
h_{\mathrm{alg}}
one can use
\mathfrak{F}(X)
instead of the whole
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{fin}}^{0}(X)
, namely,
h_{\mathrm{alg}}(\lambda)=\sup\{H_{\mathrm{alg}}(\lambda,E):E\in\mathfrak{F}(X)\}.

(4)

Also the submonoid
\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{fin}}(X)
of
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{fin}}^{0}(X)
is
S
-invariant. Since
X
is torsion precisely when
\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{fin}}(X)
is cofinal in
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{fin}}^{0}(X)
, in this case one can use
\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{fin}}(X)
for the computation of the algebraic entropy, that is,
h_{\mathrm{alg}}(\lambda)=h((\lambda_{\mathcal{P}})_{\restriction\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{fin}}(X)},{\mathfrak{s}})
for a right Følner net
{\mathfrak{s}}
for
S
; in other terms,
h_{\mathrm{alg}}(\lambda)=\sup\{H_{\mathrm{alg}}(\lambda,E):E\in\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{fin}}(X)\}
.

Remark 1.20
.

Assume that
G
is the group of left fractions of
S
and
G\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 6.0pt$\lambda$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}X
is a left
G
-action on the discrete Abelian group
X
. By Lemma
1.3
,
G
is amenable and every right Følner net for
S
is also a right Følner net for
G
.
Therefore,
h_{\mathrm{alg}}(\lambda)=h_{\mathrm{alg}}(\lambda_{\restriction S})
.

In case a subgroup
Y\leq X
is
S
-invariant, that is,
\lambda_{s}(Y)\leq Y
for all
s\in S
, one obtains the following induced left
S
-actions

S\overset{\lambda_{Y}}{\curvearrowright}Y\quad\text{and}\quad S\overset{\lambda_{X/Y}}{\curvearrowright}X/Y.

Both the inclusion
Y\to X
and the projection
X\to X/Y
are examples of
S
-equivariant homomorphisms.

For what follows we just need the following general facts about
h_{\mathrm{alg}}
. We refer to
[
10
]
for the proof of these and other general properties of the algebraic entropy, a short alternative proof along the same lines of that of Proposition
1.26
(see also Proposition
1.27
) can be used as well.

Proposition 1.21
.

In our setting, the following statements hold true:

(1)

if
S\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 6.0pt$\lambda^{\prime}$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}X^{\prime}
is a left action conjugated to
S\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 6.0pt$\lambda$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}X
, then
h_{\mathrm{alg}}(\lambda^{\prime})=h_{\mathrm{alg}}(\lambda)
;

(2)

if
Y
is an
S
-invariant subgroup of
X
, then
h_{\mathrm{alg}}(\lambda)\geq\max\{h_{\mathrm{alg}}(\lambda_{Y}),\,h_{\mathrm{alg}}(\lambda_{X/Y})\}.

The first property says that the algebraic entropy is an invariant for left
S
-actions on Abelian groups, while the second one is a monotonicity property with respect to
taking invariant subgroups and quotients, that can be seen as consequences of the Algebraic Addition Theorem.

1.6
Background on the topological entropy

In this subsection we recall the needed background about the topological entropy
h_{\mathrm{top}}(\rho)
of a right action
K\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 7.0pt$\rho$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowleft\ $}}S
of the cancellative and right amenable monoid
S
on a compact space
K
.

Given two open covers
\mathcal{U}=\{U_{i}\}_{i\in I}
and
\mathcal{V}=\{V_{j}\}_{j\in J}
of
K
, one says that
\mathcal{V}

refines

\mathcal{U}
, denoted by
\mathcal{V}\succ\mathcal{U}
, if for every
j\in J
there exists
i_{j}\in I
such that
V_{j}\subseteq U_{i_{j}}
.
For two given open covers
\mathcal{U},\mathcal{V}\in\mathrm{cov}(K)
,

\text{if}\quad\mathcal{V}\succ\mathcal{U}\quad\text{then}\quad N(\mathcal{V})\geq N(\mathcal{U}).

(1.6)

If
K^{\prime}
is a compact space,
f\colon K^{\prime}\to K
a continuous map, and
\mathcal{U}=\{U_{j}\}_{j\in J}\in\mathrm{cov}(K)
, let

f^{-1}(\mathcal{U})=\{f^{-1}(U_{j})\}_{j\in J}\in\mathrm{cov}(K^{\prime}).

Moreover, for
\mathcal{U}\in\mathrm{cov}(K)
and
F\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{fin}}(S)
, let

\mathcal{U}_{\rho,F}=\bigvee_{s\in F}\rho_{s}^{-1}(\mathcal{U}).

Definition 1.22
.

For
\mathcal{U}\in\mathrm{cov}(K)
, the
topological entropy of
\rho
with respect to
\mathcal{U}
is

H_{\mathrm{top}}(\rho,\mathcal{U})=\lim_{i\in I}\frac{\log N(\mathcal{U}_{\rho,F_{i}})}{|F_{i}|}.

(1.7)

The
topological entropy of
\rho
is
h_{\mathrm{top}}(\rho)=\sup\{H_{\mathrm{top}}(\rho,\mathcal{U}):\mathcal{U}\in\mathrm{cov}(K)\}.

For
h_{\mathrm{top}}
we present the following two alternative descriptions.

Example 1.23
.

Let
K
be a compact space.

(1)

Analogously to Example
1.19
, the assignment
K\mapsto{\mathrm{cov}}(K)
from Example
1.7
can be completed to a contravariant functor

{\mathrm{cov}}\colon{\mathbf{CompSp}}\to\mathfrak{M}

such that
{\mathrm{cov}}(f)({\cal U})=f^{-1}({\cal U})
for any morphism
f\colon K\to L
in
{\mathbf{CompSp}}
and open cover
{\cal U}\in{\mathrm{cov}}(L)
.
If
j\colon Y\to X
is a subspace embedding in
{\mathbf{CompSp}}
, the map
{\mathrm{cov}}(j)\colon\mathrm{cov}(X)\to\mathrm{cov}(Y)
is a surjective morphism in
\mathfrak{M}
, while a quotient map
q\colon X\to Y
gives rise to an embedding
\mathrm{cov}(q)\colon\mathrm{cov}(Y)\to\mathrm{cov}(X)
in
\mathfrak{M}
.

(2)

For a right
S
-action
K\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 7.0pt$\rho$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowleft\ $}}S
, the functor
\mathrm{cov}
induces a left
S
-action

S\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 6.0pt$\rho_{\mathrm{cov}}$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}\mathrm{cov}(K),\quad\text{with}\ (\rho_{\mathrm{cov}})_{s}({\cal U})=\rho_{s}^{-1}({\cal U})\ \text{for every $s\in S$ and ${\cal U}\in\mathrm{cov}(K)$}.

For any
F\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{fin}}(S)
and
{\cal U}\in\mathrm{cov}(K)
,

T_{F}(\rho_{\mathrm{cov}},{\cal U})=\bigvee_{s\in F}\rho_{s}^{-1}({\cal U})={\cal U}_{\rho,F}.

So, for any right Følner net
{\mathfrak{s}}
for
S
,

H_{\mathrm{top}}(\rho,{\cal U})=H(\rho_{\mathrm{cov}},{\mathfrak{s}},{\cal U})
and
h_{\mathrm{top}}(\rho)=h(\rho_{\mathrm{cov}},{\mathfrak{s}})
.

Hence, the existence of the limit in (
1.7
) and its independence on the choice of
{\mathfrak{s}}
follow from Lemma
1.14
.

The following example in used in §
A
.

Example 1.24
.

Let
K
be a compact group.

(1)

Consider the contravariant functor

\mathfrak{U}\colon{\mathbf{CompGr}}\to\mathfrak{M}

defined by
K\mapsto\mathfrak{U}(K)
(see Example
1.8
) and by letting
\mathfrak{U}(f)(V)=f^{-1}(V)
for every morphism
f\colon K\to L
in
{\mathbf{CompGr}}
and
V\in\mathfrak{U}(L)
.

(2)

For a right
S
-action
K\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 7.0pt$\rho$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowleft\ $}}S
, the functor
\mathfrak{U}
induces a left
S
-action

S\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 6.0pt$\rho_{\mathfrak{U}}$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}{\mathfrak{U}}(K),\quad\text{with}\ (\rho_{\mathfrak{U}})_{s}(U)=\rho_{s}^{-1}(U)\ \text{for every $s\in S$ and $U\in\mathfrak{U}(K)$}.

For any
F\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{fin}}(S)
and
U\in\mathfrak{U}(K)
,

T_{F}(\rho_{\mathfrak{U}},U)=\bigcap_{s\in F}\rho_{s}^{-1}(U)=C_{F}(\rho,U).

In these terms, for a right Følner net
{\mathfrak{s}}=\{F_{i}\}_{i\in I}
for
S
,

h(\rho_{\mathfrak{U}},{\mathfrak{s}})=\sup\{H(\rho_{\mathfrak{U}},{\mathfrak{s}},U):U\in\mathfrak{U}(K)\},\quad\text{with}\ H(\rho_{\mathfrak{U}},{\mathfrak{s}},U)=\limsup_{i\in I}\frac{-\log\mu(C_{F}(\rho,U))}{|F_{i}|}.

(3)

The submonoid
\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{o}}(K)
of
\mathfrak{U}(K)
is
S
-invariant, so
\rho_{\mathfrak{U}}
restricts to it. When
K
is profinite, that is,
\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{o}}(K)
is cofinal in
\mathfrak{U}(K)
, by Example
1.11
and Proposition
1.16
, recalling that the norm
v_{\mathfrak{U}}
restricted to
\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{o}}(K)
is subadditive and so the above limit superior becomes a limit by Lemma
1.14
, we get

h(\rho_{\mathfrak{U}},{\mathfrak{s}})=h((\rho_{\mathfrak{U}})_{\restriction\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{o}}(K)},{\mathfrak{s}})=\sup\{H(\rho_{\mathfrak{U}},{\mathfrak{s}},U):U\in\mathfrak{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{o}}(K)\},\quad\text{with}\ H(\rho_{\mathfrak{U}},{\mathfrak{s}},U)=\lim_{i\in I}\frac{\log[K:C_{F}(\rho,U)]}{|F_{i}|},

and the quantities do not depend on the choice of the right Følner net
{\mathfrak{s}}
for
S
.

(4)

Considering
\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{o}}(K)
as a submonoid of
\mathrm{cov}(K)
(as in Example
1.8
(5)),
\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{o}}(K)
is
S
-invariant also in
\mathrm{cov}(K)
, so
\rho_{\mathrm{cov}}
restricts to it and
h((\rho_{\mathrm{cov}})_{\restriction\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{o}}(K)},{\mathfrak{s}})=h((\rho_{\mathfrak{U}})_{\restriction\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{o}}(K)},{\mathfrak{s}})
for a right Følner net
{\mathfrak{s}}
for
S
.
When
K
is profinite, that is,
\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{o}}(K)
is cofinal both in
\mathfrak{U}(K)
and in
\mathrm{cov}(K)
, we then deduce that

h_{\mathrm{top}}(\rho)=h((\rho_{\mathrm{cov}})_{\restriction\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{o}}(K)},{\mathfrak{s}})=h((\rho_{\mathfrak{U}})_{\restriction\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{o}}(K)},{\mathfrak{s}})
for a right Følner net
{\mathfrak{s}}
for
S
.

(5)

When
S=G
is a group,
G\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 6.0pt$\rho_{\mathfrak{U}}$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}{\mathfrak{U}}(K)
and
G\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 6.0pt$\rho_{\mathrm{cov}}$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}{\mathrm{cov}}(K)
are equivalent (see Proposition
A.23
); hence,
h_{\mathrm{top}}(\rho)=h(\rho_{\mathfrak{U}},{\mathfrak{s}})
for any right Følner net
{\mathfrak{s}}=\{F_{i}\}_{i\in I}
for
G
, in view of Example
1.23
and Proposition
1.16
. In detail,

h_{\mathrm{top}}(\rho)=\sup\left\{\lim_{i\in I}\frac{-\log\mu(C_{F}(\rho,U))}{|F_{i}|}:U\in\mathfrak{U}(K)\right\}.

As a by-product we obtain a new proof of the well-known fact that the topological entropy defined by Adler, Konheim and McAndrew
and that defined by Bowen (namely, Hood’s extension) coincide on
{\mathbf{CompGr}}
, as mentioned in the very beginning of the introduction.

Remark 1.25
.

Let
G
be the group of left fractions of
S
and
G\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 7.0pt$\rho$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowleft\ $}}K
a right
G
-action. Then
G
is amenable and every right Følner net for
S
is also a right Følner net for
G
, by Lemma
1.3
. Therefore,
h_{\mathrm{top}}(\rho)=h_{\mathrm{top}}(\rho_{\restriction S})
.

Proposition 1.26
.

Let
K^{\prime}
be another compact space and
f\colon K\to K^{\prime}
an
S
-equivariant continuous map with respect to the right
S
-actions
K\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 7.0pt$\rho$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowleft\ $}}S
and
K^{\prime}\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 7.0pt$\rho^{\prime}$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowleft\ $}}S
.
Then, the following statements hold true:

(1)

if
f
is surjective, then
h_{\mathrm{top}}(\rho)\geq h_{\mathrm{top}}(\rho^{\prime})
;

(2)

if
f
is injective, then
h_{\mathrm{top}}(\rho)\leq h_{\mathrm{top}}(\rho^{\prime})
.

Proof.

(1) By Example
1.23
,
\mathrm{cov}(f)\colon\mathrm{cov}(K^{\prime})\to\mathrm{cov}(K)
is an embedding in
\mathfrak{M}
so
h_{\mathrm{top}}(\rho)=h(\rho_{\mathrm{cov}})\geq h(\rho^{\prime}_{\mathrm{cov}})=h_{\mathrm{top}}(\rho^{\prime})
, in view of Corollary
1.17
(1).

(2) By Example
1.23
,
\mathrm{cov}(f)
is a surjective morphism in
\mathfrak{M}
, so

h_{\mathrm{top}}(\rho)\!=\!h(\rho_{\mathrm{cov}})\!\leq\!h(\rho^{\prime}_{\mathrm{cov}})\!=\!h_{\mathrm{top}}(\rho^{\prime})
by Corollary
1.17
(2).
∎

As a consequence of the above proposition,
h_{\mathrm{top}}(\rho)=h_{\mathrm{top}}(\rho^{\prime})
, whenever the actions
\rho
and
\rho^{\prime}
are conjugated.

1.6.1
Topological entropy for linear actions on groups

Suppose now that
K
is a compact group and that the right
S
-action
K\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 7.0pt$\rho$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowleft\ $}}S
is by continuous endomorphisms. In case
H
is a closed (but not necessarily normal) subgroup of
K
which is
S
-invariant, that is,
\rho_{s}(H)\leq H
for each
s\in S
, there is an obvious right
S
-action
H\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 7.0pt$\rho_{H}$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowleft\ $}}S
that makes the inclusion
H\to K
an
S
-equivariant injective continuous homomorphism.

Denote by
\pi\colon K\to K/H=\{kH:k\in K\}
the canonical projection to the space of left
H
-cosets. These objects always satisfy the following important properties:

•

when endowed with the quotient topology,
K/H
is a compact space
[
32
, Theorems 5.21 and 5.22]
, and
\pi
is continuous, open and surjective;

•

K
acts on the left on
K/H
and
\pi
commutes with this action, i.e.,
\pi
is
K
-equivariant.

Moreover, let
K/H\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 7.0pt$\rho_{K/H}$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowleft\ $}}\ \ S
be the right action induced by
\rho
, with respect to which
\pi
is
S
-equivariant.

As a consequence of Proposition
1.26
, we obtain the following

Proposition 1.27
.

In the above notation, the following statements hold true:

(1)

if
K^{\prime}
is another compact group and
K^{\prime}\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 7.0pt$\rho^{\prime}$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowleft\ $}}S
is a right
S
-action conjugated to
K\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 7.0pt$\rho$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowleft\ $}}S
, then
h_{\mathrm{top}}(\rho^{\prime})=h_{\mathrm{top}}(\rho)
;

(2)

if
H
is a closed
S
-invariant subgroup of
K
, then
h_{\mathrm{top}}(\rho)\geq\max\{h_{\mathrm{top}}(\rho_{H}),\,h_{\mathrm{top}}(\rho_{K/H})\}
.

2
Ore localization of actions on discrete Abelian groups

Throughout this section we fix a cancellative and right amenable monoid
S
, its group of left fractions
G=S^{-1}S
, a discrete Abelian group
X
and a left
S
-action
S\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 6.0pt$\lambda$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}X
.
The aim of this section is to reduce the computation of
h_{\mathrm{alg}}(\lambda)
to the computation of the algebraic entropy of a suitable left
G
-action
G\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 6.0pt$\lambda^{*}$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}X^{*}
, called the (left) Ore localization of
S\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 6.0pt$\lambda$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}X
.

First, in §
2.1
, we construct a left
S
-action by injective endomorphisms
S\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 6.0pt$\bar{\lambda}$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}\bar{X}
and we prove that
h_{\mathrm{alg}}(\bar{\lambda})=h_{\mathrm{alg}}(\lambda)
. Then, in §
2.2
, we use
S\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 6.0pt$\bar{\lambda}$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}\bar{X}
to construct the Ore localization
G\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 6.0pt$\lambda^{*}$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}X^{*}
of
S\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 6.0pt$\lambda$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}X
, and we prove that
h_{\mathrm{alg}}(\lambda)=h_{\mathrm{alg}}(\lambda^{*})
. Finally, in §
2.3
, we sketch some categorical interpretations of the construction of the Ore localization.

2.1
Reduction to actions by injective endomorphisms

The goal of this subsection is to give an explicit construction that, starting with a left
S
-action
S\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 6.0pt$\lambda$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}X
on an Abelian group
X
, produces a new left
S
-action
S\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 6.0pt$\bar{\lambda}$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}\bar{X}
such that
\bar{\lambda}_{s}
is injective for each
s\in S
. Furthermore, we see that the algebraic entropy does not distinguish between
\lambda
and
\bar{\lambda}
, that is,
h_{\mathrm{alg}}(\lambda)=h_{\mathrm{alg}}(\bar{\lambda})
. This fact allows us to restrict our attention to the algebraic entropy of those left
S
-actions that act by injective endomorphisms, knowing that such a technical simplification causes no loss in generality.

The main ingredient in our construction is the kernel of
S\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 6.0pt$\lambda$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}X
, which is defined as follows:

\mathrm{Ker}(\lambda)=\{x\in X:\exists s\in S,\ \lambda_{s}(x)=0\}=\bigcup_{s\in S}\mathrm{Ker}(\lambda_{s}).

Lemma 2.1
.

The following statements hold true:

(1)

\mathrm{Ker}(\lambda)
is a subgroup of
X
;

(2)

\lambda_{s}^{-1}(\mathrm{Ker}(\lambda))=\mathrm{Ker}(\lambda)
, for all
s\in S
.

Proof.

(1) Consider the direct system
\{\mathrm{Ker}(\lambda_{s}):s\in S\}
of subgroups of
X
, indexed by
(S,\leq)
with the right divisibility relation. Then,
\mathrm{Ker}(\lambda)=\bigcup_{s\in S}\mathrm{Ker}(\lambda_{s})=\sum_{s\in S}\mathrm{Ker}(\lambda_{s})
, which is therefore a subgroup of
X
.

(2) Given
s,t\in S
, we have that
\lambda_{s}^{-1}(\mathrm{Ker}(\lambda_{t}))=\mathrm{Ker}(\lambda_{ts})\leq\mathrm{Ker}(\lambda)
. Furthermore,
Ss
is cofinal in
(S,\leq)
by (LO
′′
), and so
\bigcup_{t\in S}\mathrm{Ker}(\lambda_{ts})=\mathrm{Ker}(\lambda)
. Therefore,
