\lambda_{s}^{-1}(\mathrm{Ker}(\lambda))=\lambda_{s}^{-1}(\bigcup_{t\in S}\mathrm{Ker}(\lambda_{t}))=\bigcup_{t\in S}\lambda_{s}^{-1}(\mathrm{Ker}(\lambda_{t}))=\bigcup_{t\in S}\mathrm{Ker}(\lambda_{ts})=\mathrm{Ker}(\lambda).

∎

By the above lemma,
\mathrm{Ker}(\lambda)
is an
S
-invariant subgroup of
X
. Let
\bar{X}=X/\mathrm{Ker}(\lambda)
and denote by
\pi_{X}\colon X\to\bar{X}
the canonical projection. Then,
\lambda
induces a left
S
-action on
\bar{X}

S\overset{\bar{\lambda}}{\curvearrowright}\bar{X},\quad\text{such that}\quad\bar{\lambda}_{s}(\pi_{X}(x))=\bar{\lambda}_{s}(x+\mathrm{Ker}(\lambda))=\lambda_{s}(x)+\mathrm{Ker}(\lambda)=\pi_{X}(\lambda_{s}(x)),\quad\text{for all}\ s\in S,\ x\in X.

Therefore,
\pi_{X}
is
S
-equivariant.

Corollary 2.2
.

In the above setting, the following statements hold true:

(1)

\bar{\lambda}
acts on
\bar{X}
by injective endomorphisms, that is,
\bar{\lambda}_{s}
is injective for all
s\in S
;

(2)

given a second left
S
-action
S\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 6.0pt$\lambda^{\prime}$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}X^{\prime}
on an Abelian group
X^{\prime}
, and an
S
-equivariant homomorphism
\varphi\colon X\to X^{\prime}
, there is a unique homomorphism
\bar{\varphi}\colon\bar{X}\to\bar{X}^{\prime}
such that
\pi_{X^{\prime}}\circ\varphi=\bar{\varphi}\circ\pi_{X}
. Furthermore,
\bar{\varphi}
is
S
-equivariant and it is injective (resp., surjective), whenever
\varphi
is is injective (resp., surjective);

(3)

h_{\mathrm{alg}}(\bar{\lambda})\leq h_{\mathrm{alg}}(\lambda)
.

Proof.

(1) Given
s\in S
, we have that
\mathrm{Ker}(\bar{\lambda}_{s})={\pi_{X}}(\lambda_{s}^{-1}(\mathrm{Ker}({\lambda})))={\pi_{X}}(\mathrm{Ker}(\lambda))=0
, by Lemma
2.1
(2). Thus, the action of
S
on
\bar{X}
is by injective endomorphisms.

(2) The uniqueness and existence of
\bar{\varphi}
are clear, it is well-defined since
\varphi(\mathrm{Ker}(\lambda))\subseteq\mathrm{Ker}(\lambda^{\prime})
, and by construction it is
S
-equivariant. Suppose now that
\varphi
is surjective. Then,
\bar{\varphi}(\bar{X})=\bar{\varphi}(\pi_{X}(X))=\pi_{X^{\prime}}(\varphi(X))=\pi_{X^{\prime}}(X^{\prime})=\bar{X}^{\prime}
, showing that
\bar{\varphi}
is surjective. On the other hand, if
\varphi
is injective, then
\mathrm{Ker}(\bar{\varphi})=\bar{\varphi}^{-1}(\pi_{X^{\prime}}(\mathrm{Ker}(\lambda^{\prime})))=\pi_{X}(\varphi^{-1}(\mathrm{Ker}(\lambda^{\prime})))
, and so we have just to show that
\varphi^{-1}(\mathrm{Ker}(\lambda^{\prime}))\subseteq\mathrm{Ker}(\lambda)
. Indeed, assume that
x\in\varphi^{-1}(\mathrm{Ker}(\lambda^{\prime}))
. Then
\varphi(x)\in\mathrm{Ker}(\lambda^{\prime})
, so there is
s\in S
such that
\lambda^{\prime}_{s}(\varphi(x))=0
. Then
\varphi(\lambda_{s}(x))=\lambda^{\prime}_{s}(\varphi(x))=0
, which means that
\lambda_{s}(x)=0
by the injectivity of
\varphi
. Therefore,
x\in\mathrm{Ker}(\lambda)
.

(3) follows by Proposition
1.21
(2), since the projection
{\pi_{X}}\colon X\to\bar{X}
is surjective and
S
-equivariant.
∎

As a consequence of part (2) of the above corollary, the assignment
(X,\lambda)\mapsto(\bar{X},\bar{\lambda})
is part of an exact functor

\overline{(-)}\colon\mathrm{LRep}(S,{\mathbf{Ab}})\to\mathrm{LRep}(S,{\mathbf{Ab}}).

Now our aim is to sharpen the inequality in part (3) of the above corollary to the equality
h_{\mathrm{alg}}(\lambda)=h_{\mathrm{alg}}(\bar{\lambda})
.
We need first the following technical lemma:

Lemma 2.3
.

In the above setting, for each
F\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{fin}}(X)
there is an
s\in S
such that, for any choice of
x,y\in F
,

(*)\qquad\qquad\qquad{\pi_{X}}(x)={\pi_{X}}(y)\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad\lambda_{s}(x)=\lambda_{s}(y).\qquad\qquad\qquad

In particular,
|\lambda_{s}(F)|\leq|{\pi_{X}}(F)|
.

Proof.

Define a subset
A\subseteq F\times F
as follows (in fact,
A
is an equivalence relation on
F
):

A=\{(x,y)\in F\times F\colon{\pi_{X}}(x)={\pi_{X}}(y)\}.

Since
F
is finite,
A
is finite as well, so one can enumerate the elements of
A
as
A=\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\}
, with
a_{j}=(x_{j},y_{j})
for all
j=1,\ldots,n
. Now, for each
j=1,\ldots,n
, the equality
{\pi_{X}}(x_{j})={\pi_{X}}(y_{j})
holds if and only if
x_{j}-y_{j}\in\mathrm{Ker}(\lambda)
and, since
\mathrm{Ker}(\lambda)=\bigcup_{s\in S}\mathrm{Ker}(\lambda_{s})
by Lemma
2.1
(1), there is an
s_{j}\in S
with
x_{j}-y_{j}\in\mathrm{Ker}(\lambda_{s_{i}})
, that is,
\lambda_{s_{j}}(x_{j})=\lambda_{s_{j}}(y_{j})
. By Lemma
1.2
(1), one can choose
s,t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}\in S
such that
s=t_{1}s_{1}=\ldots=t_{n}s_{n}
. For each
j=1,\dots,n
, the choice of
s_{j}
,
t_{j}
and
s
allows us to show that

\lambda_{s}(x_{j})=\lambda_{t_{j}}(\lambda_{s_{j}}(x_{j}))=\lambda_{t_{j}}(\lambda_{s_{j}}(y_{j}))=\lambda_{s}(y_{j}).

Hence, we have found an
s\in S
such that
\lambda_{s}(x)=\lambda_{s}(y)
, for all
(x,y)\in A
, as desired.
∎

Proposition 2.4
.

In the above setting,
h_{\mathrm{alg}}(\lambda)=h_{\mathrm{alg}}(\bar{\lambda})
.

Proof.

The inequality
h_{\mathrm{alg}}(\bar{\lambda})\leq h_{\mathrm{alg}}(\lambda)
is proved in Corollary
2.2
.
Let
\{F_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be a right Følner net for
S
and let
E\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{fin}}(X)
.
By Lemma
2.3
applied to
T_{F_{i}}(\lambda,E)
, for each
i\in I
, there exists
s_{i}\in S
such that

|T_{s_{i}F_{i}}(\lambda,E)|=|\lambda_{s_{i}}(T_{F_{i}}(\lambda,E))|\leq|{\pi_{X}}(T_{F_{i}}(\lambda,E))|=|T_{F_{i}}(\bar{\lambda},{\pi_{X}}(E))|.

By Lemma
1.1
, the net
\{s_{i}F_{i}\}_{i\in I}
is right Følner for
S
and so:

\displaystyle H_{\mathrm{alg}}(\lambda,E)

\displaystyle=\lim_{i\in I}\frac{\log|T_{s_{i}F_{i}}(\lambda,E)|}{|s_{i}F_{i}|}\leq\lim_{i\in I}\frac{\log|T_{F_{i}}(\bar{\lambda},{\pi_{X}}(E))|}{|F_{i}|}=H_{\mathrm{alg}}(\bar{\lambda},{\pi_{X}}(E)).

Since
E
was chosen arbitrarily, we conclude that
h_{\mathrm{alg}}(\lambda)\leq h_{\mathrm{alg}}(\bar{\lambda})
.
∎

2.2
Invariance under Ore localization for
h_{\mathrm{alg}}

Given a left
S
-action
S\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 6.0pt$\lambda$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}X
on the Abelian group
X
, our first goal is to construct a canonical left
G
-action
G\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 6.0pt$\lambda^{*}$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}X^{*}
associated with
\lambda
(see Definition
2.7
). We do this in two steps: the group
X^{*}
is introduced in Definition
2.5
, while the action
\lambda^{*}
is described by its universal property in Lemma
2.6
.

Definition 2.5
.

Let
S\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 6.0pt$\bar{\lambda}$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}\bar{X}=X/\mathrm{Ker}(\lambda)
be the action induced by
\lambda
on the quotient. Consider the following direct system of Abelian groups with index set
(G,\leq_{S})
:

•

{\mathfrak{X}}=\{(X_{g},\varepsilon_{gs,g}\colon X_{gs}\to X_{g}):g\in G,\ s\in S\}
, where
X_{g}=\bar{X}
and
\varepsilon_{gs,g}=\bar{\lambda}_{s}\colon\bar{X}\to\bar{X}
, for all
s\in S
and
g\in G
;

•

denote the direct limit by
X^{*}=\varinjlim_{(G,\leq_{S})}{\mathfrak{X}}
;

•

and let
\varepsilon_{g}=\varepsilon_{g}^{X}\colon\bar{X}=X_{g}\to X^{*}
be the canonical morphism to the direct limit, for all
g\in G
.

In particular, by definition of direct limit, the following relations hold, for all
s\in S
and
g\in G
:

\varepsilon_{g}\circ\bar{\lambda}_{s}=\varepsilon_{g}\circ\varepsilon_{gs,g}=\varepsilon_{gs}.

(2.1)

Lemma 2.6
.

Let
X^{*}
and
\varepsilon_{g}\colon\bar{X}=X_{g}\to X^{*}
, for all
g\in G
, be as in Definition
2.5
. Then the following assertions hold true:

(1)

\varepsilon_{g}
is injective, for all
g\in G
. In particular, identifying
X_{g}
with the image of
\varepsilon_{g}
, we have that
X^{*}=\bigcup_{g\in G}X_{g}
;

(2)

there is a unique left
G
-action
G\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 6.0pt$\lambda^{*}$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}X^{*}
such that the following diagram commutes, for all
g,h\in G
:

\textstyle{X_{g}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}_{\bar{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon_{g}}
\textstyle{X_{gh}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon_{gh}}
\textstyle{X^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda^{*}_{g}}
\textstyle{X^{*};}

(2.2)

(3)

\varepsilon_{1}\colon\bar{X}\to X^{*}
is
S
-equivariant when we let
S
act on
\bar{X}
via
\bar{\lambda}
and on
X^{*}
via
(\lambda^{*})_{\restriction S}
;

(4)

given a second left
S
-action
S\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 6.0pt$\lambda^{\prime}$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}X^{\prime}
on an Abelian group
X^{\prime}
and an
S
-equivariant homomorphism
\varphi\colon X\to X^{\prime}
, there is a unique homomorphism
\varphi^{*}\colon X^{*}\to(X^{\prime})^{*}
such that, for every
g\in G
, the following diagram commutes

\textstyle{X^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon^{X}_{g}}
\textstyle{(X^{\prime})^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon^{X^{\prime}}_{g}}
\textstyle{\bar{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\bar{\varphi}}
\textstyle{\bar{X}^{\prime}.}

Furthermore,
\varphi^{*}
is
G
-equivariant and if
\varphi
is injective (resp., surjective) then so is
\varphi^{*}
.

Proof.

Part (1) follows from the fact that the transition maps in the direct system
\mathfrak{X}
of Definition
2.5
are all injective.

(2) Let
g\in G
and consider the following family of group homomorphisms
\{\lambda^{*}_{g,h}\colon X_{h}\to X^{*}:h\in G\}
where
X_{h}=\bar{X}
and
\lambda_{g,h}^{*}=\varepsilon_{gh}\colon\bar{X}\to X^{*}
. This family is compatible with the transition maps in
\mathfrak{X}
, in fact, for each
h\in G
and
s\in S
, we have that
\varepsilon_{ghs}=\varepsilon_{gh}\circ\bar{\lambda}_{s}
, by (
2.1
). Then, by the universal property of the direct limit, there exists a unique group homomorphism
\lambda_{g}^{*}\colon X^{*}\to X^{*}
, such that

\lambda_{g}^{*}\circ\varepsilon_{h}=\lambda^{*}_{g,h}=\varepsilon_{gh}.

(2.3)

To conclude, it is enough to verify that the family
\{\lambda_{g}^{*}:g\in G\}
is actually a left
G
-action on
X^{*}
. Indeed,
\lambda_{1}^{*}=\mathrm{id}_{X^{*}}
since
\mathrm{id}_{X^{*}}\circ\varepsilon_{h}=\varepsilon_{h}=\lambda^{*}_{1,h}
for all
h\in G
, so
\mathrm{id}_{X^{*}}
satisfies the universal property that defines
\lambda_{1}^{*}
(i.e., (
2.3
) with
g=1
). Similarly, given
g_{1},g_{2}\in G
, we have that
\lambda_{g_{1}g_{2}}^{*}=\lambda_{g_{1}}^{*}\circ\lambda_{g_{2}}^{*}
because

\lambda_{g_{1}}^{*}\circ\lambda_{g_{2}}^{*}\circ\varepsilon_{h}=\lambda_{g_{1}}^{*}\varepsilon_{g_{2}h}=\varepsilon_{g_{1}g_{2}h}=\lambda^{*}_{g_{1}g_{2},h},

for all
h\in G
. In particular,
\lambda_{g_{1}}^{*}\circ\lambda_{g_{2}}^{*}
satisfies the universal property that defines
\lambda_{g_{1}g_{2}}^{*}
(i.e., (
2.3
) with
g=g_{1}g_{2}
).

(3) Given
s\in S
, we deduce by (
2.3
) with
h=1
and
g=s
, that
\lambda_{s}^{*}\circ\varepsilon_{1}=\varepsilon_{s}
. Furthermore, by (
2.1
) with
g=1
, we deduce that
\varepsilon_{1}\circ\bar{\lambda}_{s}=\varepsilon_{s}
. Hence,
\lambda_{s}^{*}\circ\varepsilon_{1}=\varepsilon_{1}\circ\bar{\lambda}_{s}
, showing that
\varepsilon_{1}
is
S
-equivariant.

(4) Existence and uniqueness of
\varphi^{*}
are clear by the universal property of the direct limit, while
G
-equivariance follows by construction. By Corollary
2.2
(2),
\bar{\varphi}
is injective (resp., surjective) whenever
\varphi
has the same property and, therefore, one can conclude by the exactness of direct limits in
{\mathbf{Ab}}
.
∎

Definition 2.7
.

Let
S\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 6.0pt$\lambda$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}X
be a left
S
-action on the Abelian group
X
. The
(left) Ore localization
of
\lambda
is the left
G
-action

G\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 6.0pt$\lambda^{*}$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}X^{*}
, where
X^{*}
is the group introduced in Definition
2.5
and
\lambda^{*}
is the left
G
-action uniquely characterized in Lemma
2.6
(2).

By Lemma
2.6
(4), the assignment
(X,\lambda)\mapsto(X^{*},\lambda^{*})
is part of an exact functor

(-)^{*}\colon\mathrm{LRep}(S,{\mathbf{Ab}})\to\mathrm{LRep}(G,{\mathbf{Ab}}).

The rest of this subsection is devoted to the proof of the equality
h_{\mathrm{alg}}({\lambda})=h_{\mathrm{alg}}({\lambda^{*}})
, verifying the paradigm that “the algebraic entropy is invariant under Ore localization”.
Before that, we need to establish the following easy consequence of Lemma
2.6
:

Corollary 2.8
.

In the above setting, given
E\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{fin}}(X^{*})
, there is
s\in S
such that
\lambda^{*}_{s}(E)\subseteq\varepsilon_{1}(\bar{X})\leq X^{*}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
2.6
(1) we know that
X^{*}
can be written as a direct union of subgroups
X^{*}=\bigcup_{g\in G}\varepsilon_{g}(\bar{X})
and, in particular, there is some
g\in G
such that
E\subseteq\varepsilon_{g}(\bar{X})
. Write
g=s^{-1}t
with
s,t\in S
, and note that
\lambda_{s}^{*}(E)\subseteq\lambda_{s}^{*}\varepsilon_{g}(\bar{X})=\varepsilon_{t}(\bar{X})
, where the last equality follows by Lemma
2.6
(2). To conclude, take into account that
t\leq_{S}1
in
(G,\leq_{S})
, and so
\varepsilon_{t}(\bar{X})\subseteq\varepsilon_{1}(\bar{X})
.
∎

We are finally ready for the proof of the invariance of the algebraic entropy under Ore localization:

Theorem 2.9

(Invariance under Ore localization)
.

In the above setting,

h_{\mathrm{alg}}(\lambda)=h_{\mathrm{alg}}(\lambda^{*})
.

Proof.

By Proposition
2.4
, it is enough to verify that
h_{\mathrm{alg}}(\bar{\lambda})=h_{\mathrm{alg}}(\lambda^{*})
.

Given
E\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{fin}}(X^{*})
, we know by Corollary
2.8
that there exists
s\in S
such that
\lambda^{*}_{s}(E)\subseteq\varepsilon_{1}(\bar{X})\leq X^{*}
. Furthermore, by
[
10
, Lemma 2.7(b)]
, we know that if
\{F_{i}\}_{i\in I}
is a right Følner net for
S
, then so is
\{F_{i}s\}_{i\in I}
and they both are right Følner nets for
G
, by Lemma
1.3
(2). Therefore:

\displaystyle H_{\mathrm{alg}}(\lambda^{*},E)=\lim_{i\in I}\frac{\log|T_{F_{i}s}(\lambda^{*},E)|}{|F_{i}s|}=\lim_{i\in I}\frac{\log|T_{F_{i}}(\lambda^{*},\lambda^{*}_{s}(E))|}{|F_{i}s|}\overset{(*)}{=}\lim_{i\in I}\frac{\log|T_{F_{i}}(\bar{\lambda},\varepsilon_{1}^{-1}(\lambda_{s}^{*}(E)))|}{|F_{i}|}=H_{\mathrm{alg}}(\bar{\lambda},\varepsilon_{1}^{-1}(\lambda_{s}^{*}(E)))\leq h_{\mathrm{alg}}(\bar{\lambda}),

where the equality
(*)
is true since
|F_{i}|=|F_{i}s|
and since
\varepsilon_{1}
induces a bijection between
T_{F_{i}}(\bar{\lambda},\varepsilon_{1}^{-1}(\lambda_{s}^{*}(E)))
and
T_{F_{i}}(\lambda^{*},\lambda^{*}_{s}(E))
(use that
\varepsilon_{1}
is injective and
S
-equivariant by Lemma
2.6
(1) and (3)). This proves that
h_{\mathrm{alg}}(\lambda^{*})\leq h_{\mathrm{alg}}(\bar{\lambda})
.

For the proof of the converse inequality, note that the injective homomorphism
\varepsilon_{1}\colon\bar{X}\to X^{*}
is
S
-equivariant, by Lemma
2.6
(3). Hence,
h_{\mathrm{alg}}(\bar{\lambda})\leq h_{\mathrm{alg}}(\lambda^{*}_{\restriction S})
. By Remark
1.20
,
h_{\mathrm{alg}}(\lambda^{*}_{\restriction S})=h_{\mathrm{alg}}(\lambda^{*})
, therefore,
h_{\mathrm{alg}}(\bar{\lambda})\leq h_{\mathrm{alg}}(\lambda^{*})
.
∎

2.3
Categorical interpretations of the Ore localization

Given a left action
S\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 6.0pt$\lambda$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}X
on a discrete Abelian group
X
, in the previous subsections we have made the deliberate choice of giving a direct (and very explicit) construction of
S\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 6.0pt$\bar{\lambda}$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}\bar{X}
and
S\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 6.0pt$\lambda^{*}$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}X^{*}
. This approach has the advantage of making the text much more accessible and, furthermore, some degree of concreteness was needed for the proof of the equalities
h_{\mathrm{alg}}(\lambda)=h_{\mathrm{alg}}(\bar{\lambda})=h_{\mathrm{alg}}(\lambda^{*})
. On the other hand, it is also possible to express these constructions in a more categorical language and, in this way, obtain a better understanding of the whole process that, starting from
S\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 6.0pt$\lambda$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}X
, produces its Ore localization
S\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 6.0pt$\lambda^{*}$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}X^{*}
.

First of all, we observe that the category
\mathrm{LRep}(S,{\mathbf{Ab}})
is equivalent to

{\mathbb{Z}}[S]\text{-}\mathrm{Mod}
, the category of left modules over the monoid ring
{\mathbb{Z}}[S]
. In fact, a left
{\mathbb{Z}}[S]
-module
{}_{{\mathbb{Z}}[S]}X
is nothing else but an Abelian group
{}_{\mathbb{Z}}X
with a specified left
{\mathbb{Z}}[S]
-action, which is a ring homomorphism
\lambda\colon{\mathbb{Z}}[S]\to\mathrm{End}_{{\mathbf{Ab}}}(X)
, uniquely determined by the monoid homomorphism
\lambda_{\restriction S}\colon S\to\mathrm{End}_{{\mathbf{Ab}}}(X)
together with
{\mathbb{Z}}
-linearity.

Now, inside
\mathrm{LRep}(S,{\mathbf{Ab}})\cong{\mathbb{Z}}[S]\text{-}\mathrm{Mod}
, one can identify two reflective subcategories, where a full subcategory
{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}
of a category
{\mathcal{C}}
is said to be reflective if the inclusion functor
{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}\to{\mathcal{C}}
has a left adjoint functor
{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}
, called the reflector (see
[
42
, §IV.3]
for more details):

\mathrm{LRep}_{\rm bij}(S,{\mathbf{Ab}})\subseteq\mathrm{LRep}_{\rm inj}(S,{\mathbf{Ab}})\subseteq\mathrm{LRep}(S,{\mathbf{Ab}}).

Here
\mathrm{LRep}_{\rm bij}(S,{\mathbf{Ab}})
and
\mathrm{LRep}_{\rm inj}(S,{\mathbf{Ab}})
are the full subcategories of
\mathrm{LRep}(S,{\mathbf{Ab}})
of those
S\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 6.0pt$\lambda$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}X
such that each
\lambda_{s}
is bijective or injective, for all
s\in S
, respectively. Moreover,
\mathrm{LRep}_{\rm bij}(S,{\mathbf{Ab}})\cong\mathrm{LRep}(G,{\mathbf{Ab}})\cong{\mathbb{Z}}[G]\text{-}\mathrm{Mod}
, with
G=S^{-1}S
.

To verify the reflectivity of these subcategories we need to build a left adjoint to each of the two inclusion functors
\mathrm{LRep}_{\rm inj}(S,{\mathbf{Ab}})\to\mathrm{LRep}(S,{\mathbf{Ab}})
and
\mathrm{LRep}_{\rm bij}(S,{\mathbf{Ab}})\to\mathrm{LRep}(S,{\mathbf{Ab}})
. In §
2.1
and §
2.2
, we have built the corresponding reflectors “manually”, verifying that the
S
-equivariant projection
\pi_{X}\colon X\to\bar{X}
is a reflection in
\mathrm{LRep}_{\rm inj}(S,{\mathbf{Ab}})
of the left
S
-action
(S\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 6.0pt$\lambda$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}X)\in\mathrm{LRep}(S,{\mathbf{Ab}})
, while the composition
\varepsilon_{1}\circ\pi_{X}\colon X\to X^{*}
of
\pi_{X}
, followed by the
S
-equivariant injection
\varepsilon_{1}\colon\bar{X}\to X^{*}
, is a reflection of
S\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 6.0pt$\lambda$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}X
in
\mathrm{LRep}_{\rm bij}(S,{\mathbf{Ab}})
.

Alternatively, this problem can be solved via the abstract machinery of hereditary torsion pairs. Recall that, for a ring
R
, a full subcategory
\mathcal{T}
of
R\text{-}\mathrm{Mod}
is said to be a hereditary torsion class if it is closed under submodules, quotients, extensions and arbitrary direct sums. Given
\mathcal{T}
, the corresponding torsionfree class is
{\cal F}=\{M\in R\text{-}\mathrm{Mod}:\mathrm{Hom}_{R}(T,M)=0,\ \forall T\in\mathcal{T}\}
, and
\tau=(\mathcal{T},{\cal F})
is said to be a hereditary torsion pair (see
[
55
, §VI.2-3]
). In this situation,
{\cal F}
is always a reflective subcategory of
R\text{-}\mathrm{Mod}
. Moreover,
\tau
induces a so-called Gabriel topology
\mathfrak{F}_{\tau}:=\{{}_{R}I\leq R:R/I\in\mathcal{T}\}
on
R
(see
[
55
, §VI.4-5-6]
), with which we can define the corresponding Gabriel localization
(R,\mathfrak{F}_{\tau})\text{-}\mathrm{Mod}
of
R\text{-}\mathrm{Mod}
(also commonly denoted by
R\text{-}\mathrm{Mod}/\mathcal{T}
), as follows. This is the full subcategory of the
\tau_{s}
-closed (or
\tau_{s}
-local) modules, that is, those
M\in R\text{-}\mathrm{Mod}
such that the canonical map
M\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{R}(R,M)\to\mathrm{Hom}_{R}(I,M)
is an isomorphism for all
I\in\mathfrak{F}_{\tau}
(see
[
55
, §IX.1-2]
). Then,
(R,\mathfrak{F}_{\tau})\text{-}\mathrm{Mod}
is a Giraud subcategory of
R\text{-}\mathrm{Mod}
(see
[
55
, §X.1-2]
),
that is, a reflective subcategory whose reflector
Q_{\tau}\colon R\text{-}\mathrm{Mod}\to R\text{-}\mathrm{Mod}/\mathcal{T}
, called the Gabriel
\tau
-localization or
\tau
-quotient functor, is an exact functor. Being a reflection,
Q_{\tau}
is “surjective” (on isomorphism classes of objects) while, for any left
R
-module
M
,
Q_{\tau}(M)=0
if, and only if,
M\in\mathcal{T}
. Hence, we obtain a sort of “short exact sequence”
0\to\mathcal{T}\to R\text{-}\mathrm{Mod}\to R\text{-}\mathrm{Mod}/\mathcal{T}\to 0
of Abelian categories, given by the exact embedding
\mathcal{T}\to R\text{-}\mathrm{Mod}
, followed by exact
\tau
-quotient functor, where the “image” of the former is precisely the “kernel” of the latter; this explains both the notation
R\text{-}\mathrm{Mod}/\mathcal{T}
and also the fact that
Q_{\tau}
is called a quotient functor.

Let us briefly indicate how this approach can be applied to our concrete situation:

•

first of all, there is a hereditary torsion pair
\tau_{S}=(\mathcal{T}_{S},{\cal F}_{S})
in
\mathrm{LRep}(S,{\mathbf{Ab}})
, with
\mathcal{T}_{S}
the class of those actions
S\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 6.0pt$\lambda$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}X
such that
\mathrm{Ker}(\lambda)=X
, and
{\cal F}_{S}
the class of those actions
S\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 6.0pt$\lambda$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}X
such that
\mathrm{Ker}(\lambda)=0
(i.e.,
{\cal F}_{S}=\mathrm{LRep}_{\rm inj}(S,{\mathbf{Ab}})
).
Then, the inclusions
\mathcal{T}_{S}\to\mathrm{LRep}(S,{\mathbf{Ab}})
and
{\cal F}_{S}\to\mathrm{LRep}(S,{\mathbf{Ab}})
have a right and a left adjoint, respectively,

t_{S}\colon\mathrm{LRep}(S,{\mathbf{Ab}})\to\mathcal{T}_{S}\quad\text{and}\quad(1:t_{S})\colon\mathrm{LRep}(S,{\mathbf{Ab}})\to{\cal F}_{S},

(see the definitons in (
2.4
)) which are usually referred to as the
\tau_{S}
-torsion radical and the
\tau_{S}
-torsionfree coradical;

•

furthermore,
\mathrm{LRep}_{\rm bij}(S,{\mathbf{Ab}})
is a Giraud subcategory of
\mathrm{LRep}(S,{\mathbf{Ab}})
, which is equivalent to the

\tau_{S}
-localization
\mathrm{LRep}(S,{\mathbf{Ab}})/\mathcal{T}_{S}=({\mathbb{Z}}[S],\mathfrak{F}_{\tau_{s}})\text{-}\mathrm{Mod}
. In particular, the inclusion
\mathrm{LRep}_{\rm bij}(S,{\mathbf{Ab}})\to\mathrm{LRep}(S,{\mathbf{Ab}})
has the following exact left adjoint functor (that we describe explicitly below):

Q_{S}\colon\mathrm{LRep}(S,{\mathbf{Ab}})\to\mathrm{LRep}(S,{\mathbf{Ab}})/\mathcal{T}_{S}\cong\mathrm{LRep}_{\rm bij}(S,{\mathbf{Ab}}).

In fact,
\tau_{S}
is not just a hereditary torsion pair, but it is a very special and well-behaved one: the subset
S\subseteq{\mathbb{Z}}[S]
is a multiplicative subset that satisfies the left Ore condition for rings. By
[
55
, Example 2 in §VI.4 and Example in §VI.9]
, given
{}_{{\mathbb{Z}}[S]}X\in{\mathbb{Z}}[S]\text{-}\mathrm{Mod}
,

t_{S}(X)=\{x\in X:sx=0\text{ for some $s\in S$}\}\quad\text{and}\quad(1:t_{S})(X)=X/t_{S}(X).

(2.4)

This means that, in the language of left
S
-actions,
t_{S}(X)=\mathrm{Ker}(\lambda)
and
(1:t_{S})(X)=\bar{X}
. Thus, our “reduction to actions by injective endomorphisms” can be viewed as a “reduction to
\tau_{S}
-torsionfree
{\mathbb{Z}}[S]
-modules”.

Now, to construct
Q_{S}(X)
, one can first note that
{\mathbb{Z}}[G]\cong S^{-1}{\mathbb{Z}}[S]
is the ring of left
S
-fractions of
{\mathbb{Z}}[S]
. In particular, each element
r\in{\mathbb{Z}}[G]
can be written in the form
gr^{\prime}
for some
r^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{Z}}[S]
and
g\in G
. Therefore, considering
g{\mathbb{Z}}[S]
as a subgroup of
{\mathbb{Z}}[G]
in the obvious way, one can write
{\mathbb{Z}}[G]
as a direct union of Abelian groups
{\mathbb{Z}}[G]=\bigcup_{g\in G}g{\mathbb{Z}}[S]
, where there is an inclusion
\iota_{g,g^{\prime}}\colon g{\mathbb{Z}}[S]\hookrightarrow g^{\prime}{\mathbb{Z}}[S]
if, and only if,
g\leq_{S}g^{\prime}
, for all
g,g^{\prime}\in G
. Furthermore, by
[
55
, Lemma 1.6 and Example 2 in §IX.1]
,

Q_{S}(X)\cong{\mathbb{Z}}[G]\otimes_{{\mathbb{Z}}[S]}X\cong{\mathbb{Z}}[G]\otimes_{{\mathbb{Z}}[S]}(1:t_{S})(X)={\mathbb{Z}}[G]\otimes_{{\mathbb{Z}}[S]}\bar{X}.

Hence, as an Abelian group,
Q_{S}(X)
can be seen to be isomorphic to
\varinjlim_{(G,\leq_{S})}X_{g}
, where
X_{g}=g{\mathbb{Z}}[S]\otimes\bar{X}=g\otimes\bar{X}\cong\bar{X}
and where the transition maps are given by
\iota_{g,g^{\prime}}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\bar{X}}\colon X_{g}\to X_{g^{\prime}}
. Therefore,
Q_{S}(X)
is a direct limit of a direct system in
{\mathbf{Ab}}
which is isomorphic to the direct system
\mathfrak{X}
of Definition
2.5
. In particular,
Q_{S}(X)\cong X^{*}
, showing that our “reduction to actions by bijective endomorphism” can be viewed as a “reduction to
\tau_{S}
-local left
{\mathbb{Z}}[S]
-modules”.

Even if the above torsion theoretic interpretation is very neat, the theory of Gabriel localizations is specific to Abelian categories and, in fact, it works best for Grothendieck categories, which are the cocomplete Abelian categories with a generator and exact directed colimits (in fact, by a famous result of Gabriel and Popescu, an Abelian category is Grothendieck if, and only if, it is of the form
(R,\mathfrak{F}_{\tau})\text{-}\mathrm{Mod}
for a ring
R
and a hereditary torsion pair
\tau
in
R\text{-}\mathrm{Mod}
). In particular, even if we could try a similar approach for the Abelian category
\mathrm{RRep}(S,{\mathbf{CompAb}})
, one is forced to turn to a different categorical machinery in the case of
\mathrm{RRep}(S,{\mathbf{CompSp}})
.
So, let us conclude this subsection by showing that the construction of the Ore localization can also be viewed as a suitable left Kan extension (see
[
42
, §X.3]
). As Kan extensions can be defined for general functor categories, this second approach will be easy to adapt to
\mathrm{RRep}(S,{\mathbf{CompSp}})
(see §
3.4
for more details). Indeed, observe that:

•

S
and
G
can be viewed as categories with one object, say
\mathrm{Ob}(S)=\{\star\}=\mathrm{Ob}(G)
, and

\mathrm{End}_{S}(\star)=(S,\cdot,1)\subseteq(G,\cdot,1)=\mathrm{End}_{G}(\star),

where the composition is defined by the multiplication in
S
and
G
, respectively. Moreover, the inclusion
\iota\colon S\to G
can be viewed as a (non-full) inclusion of a subcategory;

•

\mathrm{LRep}(S,{\mathbf{Ab}})
and
\mathrm{LRep}(G,{\mathbf{Ab}})
can be seen as categories of covariant functors
S\to{\mathbf{Ab}}
and
G\to{\mathbf{Ab}}
, respectively. In fact, a functor
F\colon S\to{\mathbf{Ab}}
is uniquely determined by the Abelian group
X=F(\star)
and by the left
S
-action
S\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 6.0pt$\lambda$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}X
such that
\lambda_{s}=F(s)
, for all
s\in S
. Similarly, one can view covariant functors
G\to{\mathbf{Ab}}
as left
G
-actions. Therefore, the inclusion
\iota\colon S\to G
induces a forgetful functor

\iota^{*}\colon\mathrm{LRep}(G,{\mathbf{Ab}})\to\mathrm{LRep}(S,{\mathbf{Ab}}),\quad\text{such that}\quad F\mapsto F\circ\iota.

In the language of left actions, this means that
\iota^{*}
sends a left
G
-action
G\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 6.0pt$\lambda$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}X
to the left
S
-action
S\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 6.0pt$\lambda_{\restriction S}$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}X
(so just “forgetting” part of the action), that is,
\iota^{*}
is the inclusion
\mathrm{LRep}(G,{\mathbf{Ab}})\to\mathrm{LRep}(S,{\mathbf{Ab}})
introduced in §
1.3
.

When it exists, the left adjoint functor
\iota_{!}\colon\mathrm{LRep}(S,{\mathbf{Ab}})\to\mathrm{LRep}(G,{\mathbf{Ab}})
is called the left Kan extension along
\iota
. In fact, it is well-known that, whenever the target category (which, in our case, is
{\mathbf{Ab}}
) is cocomplete, left Kan extensions always exist and they can be computed pointwise using colimits (see
[
3
, Theorem 3.7.2]
or
[
42
, Theorem 1 in §X.3]
). Unraveling all the definitions, one ends up seeing (again) that, given a functor
F\colon S\to{\mathbf{Ab}}
, which corresponds uniquely to the left
S
-action
S\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 6.0pt$\lambda$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowright\ $}}X=F(\star)
, and letting
\iota_{!}(F)\colon G\to{\mathbf{Ab}}
be its left Kan extension along
\iota
, there is an isomorphism

\iota_{!}(F)(\star)\cong\varinjlim_{(G,\leq_{S})}\mathfrak{X}=X^{*},

where
\mathfrak{X}
is the direct system of Definition
2.5
. We omit the details about this isomorphism as we will be much more explicit in §
3.4
, when describing the Ore colocalization
\mathrm{RRep}(S,{\mathbf{CompSp}})\to\mathrm{RRep}(G,{\mathbf{CompSp}})
as a right Kan extension.

Remark 2.10
.

Even if the categorical treatment in this subsection may seem overly complicated, especially when compared to the natural constructions of §
2.1
and §
2.2
, it is actually an accurate illustration of the steps that we took in developing our research project. In fact, starting from Hanfeng Li’s remark that a cancellative right amenable monoid
S
is always left Ore, it did not take us too long to conclude that it was possible to use the theory of modules of left fractions (which is, essentially, the above torsion theoretic approach) to associate to any given left
S
-actions on a discrete Abelian group a canonical left
G=S^{-1}S
-action (though it took longer to prove that the process was entropy-preserving). Moreover, just formally following the dual steps (in the sense of Pontryagin-van Kampen), we readily produced a theory of Ore colocalizations for right actions on compact Abelian groups. Nevertheless, when we described the Ore localizations via suitable left Kan extensions, we obtained a purely categorical construction that applies to functor categories in general and, as usual in category theory, it comes with its formal dual. At that point, the task of defining the Ore colocalizations of actions on compact spaces was reduced to an exercise of formally inverting the direction of arrows.

The very last step in refining our theory was a bit of a technicality: the first direct system we used to build the Ore localizations in the discrete case (which was the natural choice in the context of modules of left fractions) was indexed by
S
, so it was smaller than (even if cofinal in) the one used in §
2.2
, which is indexed by
G
, and is the natural choice in the context of Kan extensions. This little change allowed for several simplifications in the arguments (e.g., in Lemma
2.6
), and it is ultimately responsible for the “natural” appearance of §
2.2
.

3
Ore colocalization of actions on compact spaces

Throughout this section we fix a cancellative and right amenable monoid
S
, its group of left fractions
G=S^{-1}S
, a compact space
K
and a right
S
-action
K\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 7.0pt$\rho$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowleft\ $}}S
.

Mirroring the results of §
2
for left actions on discrete Abelian groups, in this section we introduce first the right
S
-action
\bar{K}\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 7.0pt$\bar{\rho}$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowleft\ $}}S
by surjective continuous self-maps (see §
3.2
) and then the Ore colocalization
K^{*}\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 7.0pt$\rho^{*}$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowleft\ $}}G
of
K\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 7.0pt$\rho$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowleft\ $}}S
(see §
3.3
). Moreover, we verify the equalities
h_{\mathrm{top}}(\rho)=h_{\mathrm{top}}(\bar{\rho})=h_{\mathrm{top}}(\rho^{*})
(see Theorems
3.10
and
3.15
).

3.1
Inverse limits of compact groups and spaces

We need to recall some useful facts about inverse limits in the category
{\mathbf{CompSp}}
of compact spaces.

Consider a directed preordered set
(I,\leq)
and an inverse system in
{\mathbf{CompSp}}
:

\mathfrak{K}=\{(K_{i},\pi_{i,j}):i\geq j\ \text{in}\ I\},

(3.1)

that is, all the maps
\pi_{i,j}\colon K_{i}\to K_{j}
, with
i\geq j
in
I
, are supposed to be continuous and
\pi_{j,k}\circ\pi_{i,j}=\pi_{i,k}
, for all
i\geq j\geq k
in
I
.

In the rest of the section we analyze the structure, the topology and the open covers of the limit of
{\mathfrak{K}}
in
{\mathbf{CompSp}}
and prove some exactness-like property for the inverse limit functor in
{\mathbf{CompSp}}
.

First of all, let
\prod_{i\in I}K_{i}
be the cartesian product and denote by
p_{j}\colon\prod_{i\in I}K_{i}\to K_{j}
the canonical projection, for each
j\in I
. By the Tychonoff Theorem,
(\prod_{i\in I}K_{i},(p_{i})_{i\in I})
is a product in
{\mathbf{CompSp}}
when equipped with the product topology, generated by the pre-base:

\mathcal{B}=\{p^{-1}_{i}(U):i\in I,\,U\subseteq K_{i}\ \text{ open}\}.

Lemma 3.1
.

In the above setting, let

K^{\sharp}=\bigcap_{j\leq i\in I}\{x\in\prod_{k\in I}K_{k}:p_{j}(x)=\pi_{i,j}(p_{i}(x))\}\leq\prod_{i\in I}K_{i}

and
\pi_{j}=(p_{j})_{\restriction K^{\sharp}}\colon K^{\sharp}\to K_{j}
, for each
j\in I
. The following statements hold true:

(1)

\pi_{i,j}\pi_{i}=\pi_{j}
, for all
j\leq i
in
I
;

(2)

(K^{\sharp},(\pi_{i})_{i\in I})
is an inverse limit for
{\mathfrak{K}}
in
{\mathbf{CompSp}}
;

(3)

the family
\mathcal{B}^{*}=\{\pi_{i}^{-1}(U):i\in I,\,U\subseteq K_{i}\ \text{ open}\}
is a base for the topology of
K^{\sharp}
;

(4)

every open cover of
K^{\sharp}
has a (finite) refinement consisting of elements of
\mathcal{B}^{*}
.

Proof.

(1) is true by construction, (2) follows by
[
22
, §2.5]
and (3-4) by
[
22
, Proposition 2.5.5]
.
∎

As a consequence of the above lemma, any open subset of
K^{\sharp}
is a union (possibly infinite) of elements of
\mathcal{B}^{*}
.

Remark 3.2
.

Consider the obvious forgetful functors
{\mathbf{CompGr}}\to{\mathbf{CompSp}}
and
{\mathbf{CompAb}}\to{\mathbf{CompSp}}
, that forget the group structures and “only retain the topological information” about objects and morphisms in
{\mathbf{CompGr}}
and
{\mathbf{CompAb}}
, respectively. Given an inverse system
\mathfrak{K}
like in (
3.1
) in
{\mathbf{CompGr}}
(resp.,
{\mathbf{CompAb}}
), then the product
\prod_{i\in I}K_{i}
in
{\mathbf{CompGr}}
(resp.,
{\mathbf{CompAb}}
) is just the cartesian product with the product topology, so the above forgetful functors preserve products. Furthermore, defining
K^{\sharp}
as in Lemma
3.1
, this is a subgroup of
\prod_{i\in I}K_{i}
, so it is an object in
{\mathbf{CompGr}}
(resp.,
{\mathbf{CompAb}}
). Lifting the universal property along the forgetful functor, one concludes that
K^{\sharp}
is an inverse limit in
{\mathbf{CompGr}}
(resp.,
{\mathbf{CompAb}}
). By this observation, all the results in this subsection apply also to inverse limits in
{\mathbf{CompGr}}
(resp.,
{\mathbf{CompAb}}
).

By Lemma
3.1
, we have an explicit description of the limit
K^{\sharp}
of our inverse system
\mathfrak{K}
from (
3.1
). On the other hand, to have an even better control on the structure of
K^{\sharp}
, it is often useful to slightly modify
\mathfrak{K}
in such a way that the connecting maps become all surjective. Indeed, consider the following inverse system:

\bar{\mathfrak{K}}=\{(\bar{K}_{i},\bar{\pi}_{i,j}):i\geq j\ \text{in}\ I\},

where
\bar{K}_{i}=\mathrm{Im}(\pi_{i})\subseteq K_{i}
and
\bar{\pi}_{i,j}=(\pi_{i,j})_{\restriction\bar{K}_{i}}
, for all
i\geq j
in
I
. Then, all the connecting maps in
\bar{\mathfrak{K}}
become surjective and, furthermore, the limit of
\mathfrak{K}
and that of
\bar{\mathfrak{K}}
are the same:

Lemma 3.3
.

In the above setting, the following statements hold true:

(1)

\bar{\pi}_{i,j}
is surjective for all
i\geq j\in I
;

(2)

let
\bar{\pi}_{i}
be the corestriction to its image of
\pi_{i}\colon K^{\sharp}\to K_{i}
, for all
i\in I
. Then each
\bar{\pi}_{i}
is surjective;

(3)

(K^{\sharp},(\bar{\pi}_{i})_{i\in I})
is the limit of
\bar{\mathfrak{K}}
.

In particular,
\varprojlim{\mathfrak{K}}\cong\varprojlim{\bar{\mathfrak{K}}}
.

Proof.

See
[
22
, Proposition 2.5.6]
.
∎

The following corollary is essentially covered by
[
17
, Proposition 5.9]
; we give a direct proof for the reader’s convenience.

Corollary 3.4
.

In the above setting, let
\mathcal{U}
be a finite open cover of
K^{\sharp}
. Then there exist
i\in I
and a finite open cover
\mathcal{U}_{i}
of
K_{i}
such that
\pi_{i}^{-1}(\mathcal{U}_{i})
is a refinement of
\mathcal{U}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.1
(4), we can suppose that
\mathcal{U}=\{U_{1},\dots,U_{n}\}
, where
U_{m}=\pi_{i_{m}}^{-1}(U_{m,i_{m}})
for some open
U_{m,i_{m}}\subseteq K_{i_{m}}
, with
i_{m}\in I
, for all
m=1,\dots,n
. Using that
(I,\leq)
is directed, we can choose
i\in I
such that
i\geq i_{m}
for all
m=1,\dots,n
. Let

{\cal U}_{i}=\{\pi_{i,i_{m}}^{-1}(U_{m,i_{m}}):m=1,\dots,n\}.

By Lemma
3.1
(4), we deduce that
\pi_{i}^{-1}(\pi_{i,i_{m}}^{-1}(U_{m,i_{m}}))=\pi_{i_{m}}^{-1}(U_{m,i_{m}})=U_{m}
, for all
m=1,\dots,n
, that is,
\pi_{i}^{-1}({\cal U}_{i})={\cal U}
. We have to verify that
{\cal U}_{i}
covers
K_{i}
. Indeed,
\pi_{i}\colon K^{\sharp}\to K_{i}
is surjective by Lemma
3.3
(3), and so

K_{i}=\pi_{i}(K^{\sharp})=\pi_{i}(U_{1}\cup\ldots\cup U_{n})=\pi_{i}(U_{1})\cup\ldots\cup\pi_{i}(U_{n})=\pi_{i,i_{1}}^{-1}(U_{1,i_{1}})\cup\ldots\cup\pi_{i,i_{n}}^{-1}(U_{n,i_{n}})=\bigcup{\cal U}_{i}.\qed

We want now to analyze some “exactness” properties of inverse limits in
{\mathbf{CompSp}}
. For this consider a directed preordered set
(I,\leq)
, two inverse systems
\mathfrak{K}_{1}=\{(K^{(1)}_{i},\pi^{(1)}_{i,j}):i\geq j\ \text{in}\ I\}
,
\mathfrak{K}_{2}=\{(K^{(2)}_{i},\pi^{(2)}_{i,j}):i\geq j\ \text{in}\ I\}
and a compatible system of continuous maps
\{\varphi_{i}\colon K^{(1)}_{i}\to K^{(2)}_{i}:i\in I\}
. Finally, denote by
\varphi^{\sharp}\colon K_{1}^{\sharp}\to K_{2}^{\sharp}
the continuous map induced on the inverse limits.

Lemma 3.5
.

In the above notation, the following statements hold true:

(1)

if
\varphi_{i}
is injective for all
i\in I
, then so is
\varphi^{\sharp}
;

(2)

if
\varphi_{i}
is surjective for all
i\in I
, then so is
\varphi^{\sharp}
.

In particular, inverse limits in
{\mathbf{CompGr}}
and
{\mathbf{CompAb}}
are exact (that is, they send inverse systems of short exact sequences to short exact sequences).

Proof.

Part (1) follows by
[
22
, Lemma 2.5.9]
while (2) is
[
22
, Theorem 3.2.14]
.
∎

Let us conclude this subsection by underlining a useful technical consequence of part (2) of the above lemma, that comes handy for computations:

Corollary 3.6
.

Let
K
and
K^{\prime}
be compact Haudorff spaces,
\{H_{i}:i\in I\}
a family of closed subspaces of
K
that is downward directed by inclusion, and
\varphi\colon K\to K^{\prime}
a continuous map. Then,
\varphi(\bigcap_{i\in I}H_{i})=\bigcap_{i\in I}\varphi(H_{i})
.

Proof.

Both
\{H_{i}:i\in I\}
and
\{\varphi(H_{i}):i\in I\}
are inverse systems in
{\mathbf{CompSp}}
, and their inverse limits can be identified with
\bigcap_{i\in I}H_{i}
and
\bigcap_{i\in I}\varphi(H_{i})
, respectively. The family of surjective continuous maps
\{\varphi_{i}=\varphi_{\restriction H_{i}}\colon H_{i}\to\varphi(H_{i})\}
is compatible and so, by Lemma
3.5
(2), the induced map
\varphi^{\sharp}=\varphi_{\restriction\bigcap_{i\in I}H_{i}}\colon\bigcap_{i\in I}H_{i}\to\bigcap_{i\in I}\varphi(H_{i})
is surjective.
∎

3.2
Reduction to the case of actions by surjective maps

The
surjective core of
K\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 7.0pt$\rho$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowleft\ $}}S
is the following closed subspace of the compact space
K
:

E(\rho)=\bigcap_{t\in S}\rho_{t}(K)\subseteq K.

Lemma 3.7
.

In the above notation,
E(\rho)
is closed in
K
and
\rho_{s}(E(\rho))=E(\rho)
, for all
s\in S
.

Proof.

Since
K
is compact, each subset
\rho_{s}(K)
is compact, so closed. Therefore,
E(\rho)
is closed as well. Observe now that
Ss
is cofinal in
(S,\leq)
by (LO
′′
), so that
E(\rho)=\bigcap_{t\in S}\ \rho_{ts}(K)
. Furthermore, by Corollary
3.6
,

\rho_{s}(E(\rho))=\rho_{s}\left(\bigcap_{t\in S}\rho_{t}(K)\right)\overset{}{=}\bigcap_{t\in S}\rho_{s}\rho_{t}(K)=\bigcap_{ts\in Ss}\rho_{ts}(K)=E(\rho).\qed

By the above lemma,
E(\rho)
is a closed
S
-invariant subspace of
K
. Let
\bar{K}=E(\rho)
, denote by
\varepsilon_{K}\colon E(\rho)\to K
the inclusion map, and let

\bar{K}\overset{\bar{\rho}}{\curvearrowleft}S,\quad\text{such that}\ \bar{\rho}_{s}=(\rho_{s})_{\restriction\bar{K}}\ \text{for all}\ s\in S.

Corollary 3.8
.

In the above setting, the following statements hold true:

(1)

\bar{\rho}
acts on
\bar{K}
by surjective continuous self-maps, that is,
\bar{\rho}_{s}
is surjective for all
s\in S
;

(2)

given a second right
S
-action
K^{\prime}\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 7.0pt$\rho^{\prime}$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowleft\ $}}S
on the compact space
K^{\prime}
, and an
S
-equivariant continuous map
\varphi\colon K\to K^{\prime}
, there is a unique continuous map
\bar{\varphi}\colon\bar{K}\to\bar{K}^{\prime}
such that
\varphi\circ\varepsilon_{K}=\varepsilon_{K^{\prime}}\circ\bar{\varphi}
. Furthermore,
\bar{\varphi}
is
S
-equivariant and it is injective (resp., surjective), whenever
\varphi
is is injective (resp., surjective);

(3)

h_{\mathrm{top}}(\bar{\rho})\leq h_{\mathrm{top}}(\rho)
.

Proof.

(1) It is clear from Lemma
3.7
that
\bar{\rho}
acts by surjective self-maps.

(2) Using Corollary
3.6
, we conclude that

\varphi(\bar{K})=\varphi\left(\bigcap_{s\in S}\rho_{s}(K)\right)=\bigcap_{s\in S}\varphi(\rho_{s}(K))=\bigcap_{s\in S}\rho^{\prime}_{s}(\varphi(K^{\prime}))\subseteq\bigcap_{s\in S}\rho^{\prime}_{s}(K^{\prime})=\bar{K}^{\prime}.

(3.2)

Hence,
\varphi
restricts to a unique continuous map
\bar{\varphi}=\varphi_{\restriction\bar{K}}\colon\bar{K}\to\bar{K}^{\prime}
, that is clearly
S
-equivariant. Furthermore, if
\varphi
is injective, its restriction
\bar{\varphi}
is also injective. On the other hand, if
\varphi
is surjective, then for each
s\in S
we have that
\varphi(\rho_{s}(K))=\rho_{s}^{\prime}(\varphi(K))=\rho_{s}^{\prime}(K^{\prime})
. Exploiting this equality we can deduce that the last inclusion in (
3.2
) becomes an equality, so
\bar{\varphi}(\bar{K})=\varphi(\bar{K})=\bigcap_{s\in S}\rho^{\prime}_{s}(K^{\prime})=\bar{K}^{\prime}
.

(3) This inequality follows by Proposition
1.27
(2), as the inclusion
\varepsilon_{K}\colon\bar{K}\to K
is injective and
S
-equivariant.
∎

By part (2) of the above corollary, the assignment
(K,\rho)\mapsto(\bar{K},\bar{\rho})
is part of a functor

\overline{(-)}\colon\mathrm{RRep}(S,{\mathbf{CompSp}})\to\mathrm{RRep}(S,{\mathbf{CompSp}}),

(3.3)

that sends continuous injective (resp., surjective)
S
-equivariant maps to continuous injective (resp., surjective)
S
-equivariant maps, respectively. Furthermore, this functor restricts to exact functors

\overline{(-)}\colon\mathrm{RRep}(S,{\mathbf{CompGr}})\to\mathrm{RRep}(S,{\mathbf{CompGr}})\quad\text{and}\quad\overline{(-)}\colon\mathrm{RRep}(S,{\mathbf{CompAb}})\to\mathrm{RRep}(S,{\mathbf{CompAb}}).

Now our aim is to upgrade the inequality in Corollary
3.8
(3) to the equality
h_{\mathrm{top}}(\rho)=h_{\mathrm{top}}(\bar{\rho})
. First we need the following technical lemma:

Lemma 3.9
.

In the above notation, given a finite open cover
{\cal U}
of
K
, there exists
s\in S
such that

N_{E(\rho)}({\cal U})=N_{\rho_{s}(K)}({\cal U})=N_{K}(\rho_{s}^{-1}({\cal U})).

(3.4)

Proof.

The second equality is clear, we prove the first one.
It is clear that
N_{\rho_{t}(K)}({\cal U})\leq N_{K}({\cal U})
, for each
t\in S
. Therefore,
\{N_{\rho_{t}(K)}({\cal U}):t\in S\}\subseteq\{1,2,\ldots,N_{K}({\cal U})\}
and so there exists
s\in S
such that

N_{\rho_{s}(K)}({\cal U})=\min\{N_{\rho_{t}(K)}({\cal U}):t\in S\}.

(3.5)

Let us show that this
s
verifies (
3.4
). Indeed, pick a subfamily
{\cal V}
of
{\cal U}
that covers
E(\rho)
and such that
N_{E(\rho)}({\cal U})=|{\cal V}|
.
Then the subset
A=\bigcup{\cal V}
of
K
is open. Since
E(\rho)=\bigcap_{t\in S}\rho_{t}(K)\subseteq A
and
K
is compact, there is a finite subset
F\subseteq S
such that
E(\rho)\subseteq\bigcap_{t\in F}\rho_{t}(K)\subseteq A
. As
\{\rho_{t}(K):t\in S\}
is an inverse system, there exists a single
t\in S
with

E(\rho)\subseteq\rho_{t}(K)\subseteq A
.
Therefore,
N_{E(\rho)}({\cal U})\geq N_{\rho_{t}(K)}({\cal U})\geq N_{\rho_{s}(K)}({\cal U})
, by the choice of
{\cal V}
,
A
and (
3.5
). The converse inequality
N_{\rho_{s}(K)}({\cal U})\geq N_{E(\rho)}({\cal U})
follows by the inclusion
E(\rho)\subseteq\rho_{s}(K)
.
∎

We are ready to show that the computation of the topological entropy can be reduced to actions by surjective self-maps:

Theorem 3.10
.

In the above notation,
h_{\mathrm{top}}(\rho)=h_{\mathrm{top}}(\bar{\rho})
.

Proof.

In view of Corollary
3.8
, it is enough to see that
h_{\mathrm{top}}(\rho)\leq h_{\mathrm{top}}(\bar{\rho})
. Hence, let
\{F_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be a right Følner net for
S
,
{\cal U}\in\mathrm{cov}(K)
and, for every
i\in I
, let
{\cal V}_{i}={\cal U}_{\rho,F_{i}}
. By Lemma
3.9
there exists
s_{i}\in S
such that

N_{K}(\rho_{s_{i}}^{-1}({\cal V}_{i}))=N_{E(\rho)}({\cal V}_{i}).

(3.6)

Next we observe that

\rho_{s_{i}}^{-1}({\cal V}_{i})=\rho_{s_{i}}^{-1}\left(\bigvee_{f\in F_{i}}\rho_{f}^{-1}({\cal U})\right)=\bigvee_{f\in F_{i}}\rho_{s_{i}}^{-1}(\rho_{f}^{-1}({\cal U}))=\bigvee_{f\in F_{i}}(\rho_{f}\rho_{s_{i}})^{-1}({\cal U})=\bigvee_{f\in F_{i}}\rho_{s_{i}f}^{-1}({\cal U})={\cal U}_{\rho,s_{i}F_{i}}.

The latter equality and (
3.6
) give

N_{K}({\cal U}_{\rho,s_{i}F_{i}})=N_{E(\rho)}({\cal U}_{\rho,F_{i}}).

(3.7)

The net
\{s_{i}F_{i}\}_{i\in I}
is right Følner by Lemma
1.1
with
|s_{i}F_{i}|=|F_{i}|
(as
S
is cancellative), so, from (
3.7
), we get:

H_{\mathrm{top}}(\rho,{\cal U})=\lim_{i\in I}\frac{\log N_{K}({\cal U}_{\rho,s_{i}F_{i}})}{|s_{i}F_{i}|}=\lim_{i\in I}\frac{\log N_{E(\rho)}({\cal U}_{\rho,F_{i}})}{|F_{i}|}=H_{\mathrm{top}}(\bar{\rho},{\cal U})\leq h_{\mathrm{top}}(\bar{\rho}).

Since
{\cal U}\in\mathrm{cov}(K)
was chosen arbitrarily, we deduce that
h_{\mathrm{top}}(\rho)\leq h_{\mathrm{top}}(\bar{\rho})
, as desired.
∎

Remark 3.11
.

According to the well-known Halmos’ paradigm, an endomorphism of a compact group is measure-preserving with respect to the Haar measure if and only if it is surjective.
Therefore, when applied to a right action
K\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 7.0pt$\rho$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowleft\ $}}S
on a compact group
K
, the above theorem suggests how to pass from
\rho

to the continuous and measure-preserving right
S
-action
E(\rho)\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 7.0pt$\bar{\rho}$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowleft\ $}}S
. So, one can also discuss the measure entropy of such an action; it is known that for actions of amenable countable groups on compact metrizable groups the topological and the measure entropy coincide
[
37
, Proposition 13.2]
.

3.3
Invariance under Ore colocalization for
h_{\mathrm{top}}

We are now going to modify the right
S
-action
\bar{K}\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 7.0pt$\bar{\rho}$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowleft\ $}}S
introduced in the previous section to make it into a new action by invertible maps:

Lemma 3.12
.

Consider the following inverse system
{\mathfrak{K}}=\{(K_{g},\bar{\rho}_{s}\colon K_{g}\to K_{gs}):g\in G,\ s\in S\}
, where
K_{g}=\bar{K}
for all
g\in G
, and let
K^{*}=\varprojlim_{(G,\leq_{S})}\mathfrak{K}
, with
\pi_{g}=\pi_{g}^{K}\colon K^{*}\to K_{g}
the canonical map from the inverse limit, for all
g\in G
. Then:

(1)

\pi_{g}
is surjective and
\pi_{gs}=\bar{\rho}_{s}\circ\pi_{g}
, for all
g\in G
and
s\in S
;

(2)

let
\rho^{*}_{g}\colon K^{*}\to K^{*}
be the unique continuous map such that the following diagram commutes for all
g,h\in G
:

\textstyle{K_{gh}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}_{\bar{K}}}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{gh}}
\textstyle{K_{h}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{h}}
\textstyle{K^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho^{*}_{g}}
\textstyle{K^{*}.}

(3.8)

Then,
K^{*}\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 7.0pt$\rho^{*}$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowleft\ $}}G
is a right
G
-action;

(3)

\pi_{1}\colon K^{*}\to\bar{K}
is (surjective and)
S
-equivariant, when
K^{*}
is endowed with the restriction
(\rho^{*})_{\restriction S}
of the action
\rho^{*}
to
S
;

(4)

given a second right
S
-action
K^{\prime}\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 7.0pt$\rho^{\prime}$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowleft\ $}}S
on a compact space
K^{\prime}
and an
S
-equivariant continuous map
\varphi\colon K\to K^{\prime}
, there is a unique continuous map
\varphi^{*}\colon K^{*}\to(K^{\prime})^{*}
such that, for every
g\in G
, the following diagram commutes

\textstyle{K^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\pi^{K}_{g}}
\textstyle{(K^{\prime})^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi^{K^{\prime}}_{g}}
\textstyle{\bar{K}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\bar{\varphi}}
\textstyle{\bar{K}^{\prime}.}

Furthermore,
\varphi^{*}
is
G
-equivariant and if
\varphi
is injective (resp., surjective) then so is
\varphi^{*}
.

Proof.

(1) follows by Lemma
3.1
.

(2) Given
g\in G
, the family of maps
\{\pi_{gh}\colon K^{*}\to K_{h}:h\in G\}
is compatible with the transition maps in
\mathfrak{K}
. Hence, there is a unique continuous map
\rho^{*}_{g}\colon K^{*}\to K^{*}
such that

\pi_{h}\circ\rho^{*}_{g}=\pi_{gh},\quad\text{for all $h\in G$.}

(3.9)

Let us show that this defines a right action
K\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 7.0pt$\rho^{*}$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowleft\ $}}G
. Indeed,
\rho_{1}^{*}=\mathrm{id}_{K^{*}}
since
\pi_{h}\circ\mathrm{id}_{K^{*}}=\pi_{h}
holds for all
h\in G
, that is,
\mathrm{id}_{K^{*}}
satisfies the universal property (
3.9
) with
g=1
. Similarly, given
g_{1},g_{2}\in G
, we have that
\rho_{g_{1}}^{*}\circ\rho_{g_{2}}^{*}=\rho_{g_{2}g_{1}}^{*}
, in fact, the equalities

\pi_{h}\circ(\rho_{g_{1}}^{*}\circ\rho_{g_{2}}^{*})=(\pi_{h}\circ\rho_{g_{1}}^{*})\circ\rho_{g_{2}}^{*}=\pi_{g_{1}h}\circ\rho_{g_{2}}^{*}=\pi_{(g_{2}g_{1})h}

hold for all
h\in G
. In particular,
\rho_{g_{1}}^{*}\circ\rho_{g_{2}}^{*}
satisfies the universal property (
3.9
) with
g=g_{2}g_{1}
.

(3) For each
s\in S
and
x\in K^{*}
, we have that
(\pi_{1}\circ\rho^{*}_{s})(x)=\pi_{s}(x)={(\bar{\rho}_{s}\circ\pi_{1})}(x)
, where the former equality comes from the commutative diagram (
3.8
) with
h=1
and
g=s
, while the latter comes from part (1) with
g=1
.

(4) The existence and uniqueness of
\varphi^{*}
are clear by the universal property of inverse limits, while
\varphi^{*}
is clearly
G
-equivariant
by construction. Furthermore, by Corollary
3.8
(2),
\bar{\varphi}
is injective (resp., surjective) whenever
\varphi
has the same property. One can then conclude by Lemma
3.5
.
∎

Definition 3.13
.

The
(left) Ore colocalization
of
K\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 7.0pt$\rho$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowleft\ $}}S
is the right
G
-action
K^{*}\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 7.0pt$\rho^{*}$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowleft\ $}}G
on the compact space
K^{*}
, built in Lemma
3.12
.

Let us remark that, by part (4) of the above lemma, the assignment
(K,\rho)\mapsto(K^{*},\rho^{*})
is part of a functor

{(-)^{*}}\colon\mathrm{RRep}(S,{\mathbf{CompSp}})\to\mathrm{RRep}(G,{\mathbf{CompSp}}),

(3.10)

that sends continuous injective (resp., surjective)
S
-equivariant maps to continuous injective (resp., surjective)
G
-equivariant maps.
Furthermore, this functor restricts to exact functors

{(-)^{*}}\colon\mathrm{RRep}(S,{\mathbf{CompGr}})\to\mathrm{RRep}(G,{\mathbf{CompGr}})\quad\text{and}\quad{(-)^{*}}\colon\mathrm{RRep}(S,{\mathbf{CompAb}})\to\mathrm{RRep}(G,{\mathbf{CompAb}}).

In fact, the Ore colocalization is even “exact” on “generalized short exact sequence” of the form
H\hookrightarrow K\twoheadrightarrow K/H
, where
K
is a compact group with a right
S
-action,
H
is an
S
-invariant closed subgroup, and
K/H
is the
K
-space of left
H
-cosets.

Corollary 3.14
.

Let
K\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 7.0pt$\rho$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowleft\ $}}S
be a right
S
-action on a compact group
K
, let
H\leq K
be a closed
S
-invariant subgroup and let
H\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 7.0pt$\rho_{H}$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowleft\ $}}S
and
K/H\hskip 5.0pt\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{6.02773pt}{\hskip-2.5pt$\rho_{K/H}$}}}{\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\ \curvearrowleft\ $}}\hskip 6.0ptS
be the right
S
-actions (by continuous endomorphisms, and by continuous self-maps, respectively) induced by
\rho
on
H
and on
K/H
, respectively. If we denote by
\iota\colon H\to K
the inclusion and by
\pi\colon K\to K/H
the projection, then:

(1)

\iota^{*}\colon H^{*}\to K^{*}
is an injective,
G
-equivariant, continuous and closed group homomorphism, i.e., the action
H^{*}\hskip 5.0pt\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{6.02773pt}{\hskip-2.5pt$\ \ (\rho_{H})^{*}$}}}{\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\ \curvearrowleft\ $}}\hskip 6.0ptG
is conjugated to the action
\iota^{*}(H^{*})\hskip 5.0pt\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{6.02773pt}{\hskip-2.5pt$(\rho^{*})_{\iota^{*}(H^{*})}$}}}{\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\ \curvearrowleft\ $}}\hskip 6.0pt\ G
;

(2)

\pi^{*}\colon K^{*}\to(K/H)^{*}
is a surjective,
G
-equivariant, continuous and open map;
moreover, the action
(K/H)^{*}\hskip 5.0pt\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{6.02773pt}{\hskip-2.5pt$(\rho_{K/H})^{*}$}}}{\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\ \curvearrowleft\ $}}\hskip 6.0ptG
is conjugated to the action
K^{*}/H^{*}\hskip 5.0pt\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{6.02773pt}{\hskip-2.5pt$(\rho^{*})_{K^{*}/H^{*}}$}}}{\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\ \curvearrowleft\ $}}\hskip 6.0pt\ \ G
induced by
\rho^{*}
on the space of left
H^{*}
-cosets.

Proof.

Recall that
\iota\colon H\to K
is an
S
-equivariant closed embedding and that
\pi\colon K\to K/H
is an
S
-equivariant surjective continuous and open map.

(1) According to Lemma
3.12
(4), the continuous
G
-equivariant homomorphism
\iota^{*}\colon H^{*}\to K^{*}
is injective, since
\iota
is injective. Closedness follows from the compactness of
H^{*}
.

(2) According to Lemma
3.12
(4), the continuous
G
-equivariant homomorphism
\pi^{*}
is surjective, since
\pi
is surjective.
Openness of
\pi^{*}
follows from the open mapping theorem which applies to all compact groups
[
2
, Corollary 8.4.2]
.
The second part of (2) follows from the first one.
∎

Let us conclude this section by proving that the topological entropy “is invariant under Ore colocalization”:

Theorem 3.15

(Invariance under Ore colocalization)
.

In the above notation,
h_{\mathrm{top}}(\rho)=h_{\mathrm{top}}(\rho^{*})
.

Proof.

By Theorem
3.10
, it is enough to verify that
h_{\mathrm{top}}(\bar{\rho})=h_{\mathrm{top}}(\rho^{*})
.
Fix, all along this proof, a right Følner net
\{F_{i}\}_{i\in I}
for
S
, so that,
\{F_{i}\}_{i\in I}
is also a right Følner net for
G
, by Lemma
1.3
(2).

Let
{\cal U}\in\mathrm{cov}(K^{*})
. By Corollary
3.4
, there exist
s\in S
and a finite open cover
{\cal V}
of
K_{s}=\bar{K}
such that
{\cal U}=\pi_{s}^{-1}({\cal V})
.
We then have the following chain of equalities:

\rho^{*}_{s}({\cal U})=\rho^{*}_{s}(\pi_{s}^{-1}({\cal V}))=(\pi_{s}\circ\rho^{*}_{s^{-1}})^{-1}({\cal V})\overset{{(*)}}{=}\pi_{1}^{-1}({\cal V}),

(3.11)

where
(*)
follows since
\pi_{s}\circ\rho^{*}_{s^{-1}}=\pi_{1}
(see (
3.8
) with
g=s^{-1}
and
h=s
).
By
[
10
, Lemma 2.7(b)]
,
\{F_{i}s^{-1}\}_{i\in I}
is a right Følner net for
G
. Now, for each
i\in I
,

\displaystyle{\cal U}_{\rho^{*},F_{i}s^{-1}}

\displaystyle=\bigvee_{f\in F_{i}}(\rho^{*}_{fs^{-1}})^{-1}({\cal U})=\bigvee_{f\in F_{i}}(\rho^{*}_{f})^{-1}\circ(\rho^{*}_{s^{-1}})^{-1}({\cal U})=\bigvee_{f\in F_{i}}(\rho^{*}_{f})^{-1}(\rho^{*}_{s}({\cal U}))=(\rho^{*}_{s}({\cal U}))_{\rho^{*},F_{i}}.

(3.12)

Furthermore, using that
\pi_{1}\circ\rho_{s}^{*}=\bar{\rho}_{s}\circ\pi_{1}
, for all
s\in S
(by Lemma
3.12
(3)), we obtain that:

\displaystyle(\pi_{1}^{-1}({\cal V}))_{\rho^{*},F_{i}}=\bigvee_{f\in F_{i}}(\rho^{*}_{f})^{-1}(\pi_{1}^{-1}({\cal V}))=\bigvee_{f\in F_{i}}\pi_{1}^{-1}((\bar{\rho}_{f})^{-1}({\cal V}))=\pi_{1}^{-1}\left(\bigvee_{f\in F_{i}}(\bar{\rho}_{f})^{-1}({\cal V})\right)=\pi_{1}^{-1}({\cal V}_{\bar{\rho},F_{i}}).

(3.13)

Now, the chains of equalities (
3.13
) and (
3.12
) can be connected via (
3.11
):

{\cal U}_{\rho^{*},F_{i}s^{-1}}\ \overset{\eqref{last-inch}}{=}\ (\rho^{*}_{s}({\cal U}))_{\rho^{*},F_{i}}\ \overset{\eqref{Eq**:Feb16}}{=}\ (\pi_{1}^{-1}({\cal V}))_{\rho^{*},F_{i}}\ \overset{\eqref{very_last-inch}}{=}\ \pi_{1}^{-1}({\cal V}_{\bar{\rho},F_{i}}).

Hence,
N({\cal U}_{\rho^{*},F_{i}s^{-1}})=N(\pi_{1}^{-1}({\cal V}_{\bar{\rho},F_{i}}))=N({\cal V}_{\bar{\rho},F_{i}})
, where the second equality uses the surjectivity of
\pi_{1}
. By the above equalities,

H_{\mathrm{top}}(\rho^{*},{\cal U})=\lim_{i\in I}\frac{\log N({\cal U}_{\rho^{*},F_{i}s^{-1}})}{|F_{i}s^{-1}|}=\lim_{i\in I}\frac{\log N({\cal V}_{\bar{\rho},F_{i}})}{|F_{i}|}=H_{\mathrm{top}}(\bar{\rho},{\cal V})\leq h_{\mathrm{top}}(\bar{\rho}).

Therefore, one gets
h_{\mathrm{top}}(\rho^{*})\leq h_{\mathrm{top}}(\bar{\rho})
.

By Lemma
3.12
(3),
\pi_{1}\colon K^{*}\to\bar{K}
is (surjective and)
S
-equivariant, hence
h_{\mathrm{top}}(\rho^{*}_{\restriction S})\geq h_{\mathrm{top}}(\bar{\rho})
by Proposition
1.26
(1). On the other hand,
h_{\mathrm{top}}(\rho^{*})=h_{\mathrm{top}}(\rho^{*}_{\restriction S})
, by Remark
1.25
. This proves that
h_{\mathrm{top}}(\rho^{*})\geq h_{\mathrm{top}}(\bar{\rho})
.
∎

3.4
Categorical interpretation of the Ore colocalization

As announced in §
2.3
, it is possible to give a categorical interpretation of the Ore colocalization by using the machinery of right Kan extensions (see
[
3
, §3.7]
or
[
42
, §X.3]
). Indeed, consider
S
and
G
as categories with one object (called
\star
, as in §
2.3
) and note that
\mathrm{RRep}(S,{\mathbf{CompSp}})
and
\mathrm{RRep}(G,{\mathbf{CompSp}})
can be seen as categories of contravariant functors
S\to{\mathbf{CompSp}}
and
G\to{\mathbf{CompSp}}
, respectively. In fact, a functor
F\colon S\to{\mathbf{CompSp}}
is uniquely determined by the compact space
K=F(\star)
and by the right
S
-action
K\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 7.0pt$\rho$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowleft\ $}}S
such that
\rho_{s}=F(s)
, for all
s\in S
. Similarly, one can view any contravariant functor
F^{\prime}\colon G\to{\mathbf{CompSp}}
as a right
G
-action on the compact space
F^{\prime}(\star)
.

As usual, it is convenient to identify the categories of contravariant functors
\mathrm{RRep}(S,{\mathbf{CompSp}})
and
\mathrm{RRep}(G,{\mathbf{CompSp}})
with the categories of covariant functors
\mathrm{LRep}(S^{\mathrm{o}},{\mathbf{CompSp}})
and
\mathrm{LRep}(G^{\mathrm{o}},{\mathbf{CompSp}})
, respectively, where
{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathrm{o}}
denotes the opposite of a given category
{\mathcal{C}}
.

Therefore, the inclusion
\iota^{\mathrm{o}}\colon S^{\mathrm{o}}\to G^{\mathrm{o}}
induces a forgetful functor

\iota^{*}\colon\mathrm{LRep}(G^{\mathrm{o}},{\mathbf{CompSp}})\to\mathrm{LRep}(S^{\mathrm{o}},{\mathbf{CompSp}}),\quad\text{such that}\quad F\mapsto F\circ\iota^{\mathrm{o}}.

In the language of right actions, this means that
\iota^{*}
sends a right
G
-action
K\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 7.0pt$\rho$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowleft\ $}}G
to the right
S
-action
K\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 7.0pt$\rho_{\restriction S}$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowleft\ $}}S
(which just “forgets” part of the action), that is,
\iota^{*}
is naturally isomorphic to the inclusion
\mathrm{RRep}(G,{\mathbf{CompSp}})\to\mathrm{RRep}(S,{\mathbf{CompSp}})
.

By the dual of
[
3
, Theorem 3.7.2]
(see also
[
42
, Theorem 1 in §X.3]
), and since
{\mathbf{CompSp}}
has all limits, the functor
\iota^{*}
has a right adjoint functor:

\iota_{*}\colon\mathrm{LRep}(S^{\mathrm{o}},{\mathbf{CompSp}})\to\mathrm{LRep}(G^{\mathrm{o}},{\mathbf{CompSp}}).

This right adjoint
\iota_{*}
to the forgetful functor is called the right Kan extension along
\iota^{\mathrm{o}}
. In fact, given a covariant functor
F\colon S^{\mathrm{o}}\to{\mathbf{CompSp}}
, which corresponds uniquely to the right
S
-action
F(\star)=(K\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 7.0pt$\rho$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowleft\ $}}S)
, there is an explicit construction of the right Kan extension
\iota_{*}(F)\colon G^{\mathrm{o}}\to{\mathbf{CompSp}}
as the limit of a suitable inverse system, indexed by a specific comma category (for the general result, see
[
42
, Theorem 1 in §X.3]
). In what follows we suggest how to recover our construction of
K^{*}\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 7.0pt$\rho^{*}$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowleft\ $}}G
in §
3.2
and
3.3
from the general theory of the right Kan extensions.

In other words, we show that the action
K^{*}\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 7.0pt$\rho^{*}$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowleft\ $}}G
is the right Kan extension of
K\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 7.0pt$\rho$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowleft\ $}}S
or, equivalently, that
K^{*}\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 7.0pt$\rho^{*}$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowleft\ $}}G
, together with the
S
-equivariant morphism
K^{*}\to K
obtained as a composition of the projection
\pi_{1}\colon K^{*}\to\bar{K}
and the inclusion
\bar{K}\to K
, is a coreflection of
(K\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 7.0pt$\rho$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowleft\ $}}S)\in\mathrm{RRep}(S,{\mathbf{CompSp}})
in
\mathrm{RRep}(G,{\mathbf{CompSp}})
.

We start recalling the pointwise construction of the right Kan extension of a functor
F\colon S\to{\mathbf{CompSp}}
as a limit:

•

consider the comma category
\star/\iota^{\mathrm{o}}
, that is a category with objects
\mathrm{Ob}(\star/\iota^{\mathrm{o}})=\{(\star,g):g\in\mathrm{End}_{G^{\mathrm{o}}}(\star)\}
and where a morphism
(\star,g)\to(\star,g^{\prime})
is just a morphism
s\in\mathrm{End}_{S^{\mathrm{o}}}(\star)
such that
sg=g^{\prime}
(in
G^{\mathrm{o}}
);

•

we denote by
p\colon\star/\iota^{\mathrm{o}}\to S^{\mathrm{o}}
the projection onto the first component of the objects in
\star/\iota^{\mathrm{o}}
, and we take the composition
F\circ p\colon\star/\iota^{\mathrm{o}}\to{\mathbf{CompSp}}
;

•

one can then compute the right Kan extension via the following limit

\iota_{*}(F)(\star)\cong\lim{}_{\star/\iota^{\mathrm{o}}}(F\circ p).

Let us try to make the above construction more explicit: the category
\star/\iota^{\mathrm{o}}
is easily seen to be equivalent to the category
(G,\leq_{S})^{\mathrm{o}}
, such that
\mathrm{Ob}((G,\leq_{S})^{\mathrm{o}})=\{g:g\in G\}
and

\mathrm{Hom}_{(G,\leq_{S})^{\mathrm{o}}}(g,g^{\prime})=\begin{cases}\{s\}&\text{if, and only if, $g^{\prime}=gs$ in $G$;}\\
\emptyset&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

In particular,
\mathrm{Hom}_{(G,\leq_{S})^{\mathrm{o}}}(g,g^{\prime})
has one element precisely when
g^{\prime}\leq_{S}g
, while it is empty otherwise. Furthermore, the functor
p\colon(G,\leq_{S})^{\mathrm{o}}\to S^{\mathrm{o}}
sends each object
g\in\mathrm{Ob}((G,\leq_{S})^{\mathrm{o}})
to the unique object
\star\in\mathrm{Ob}(S^{\mathrm{o}})
, while it sends the unique morphism
s\in\mathrm{Hom}_{(G,\leq_{S})^{\mathrm{o}}}(g,gs)
to the endomorphism
p(s)=s\in\mathrm{End}_{S^{\mathrm{o}}}(\star)
.

Therefore, the diagram
F\circ p\colon(G,\leq_{S})^{\mathrm{o}}\to{\mathbf{CompSp}}
is such that
(F\circ p)(g)=K
for all
g\in G
. Furthermore, for each
g\in G
and
s\in S
, we have that
(F\circ p)(s\colon g\to gs)=(\rho_{s}\colon K\to K)
. Hence,
\iota_{*}(F)(\star)
is the inverse limit in
{\mathbf{CompSp}}
of an inverse system of copies of
K
, indexed by
(G,\leq_{S})
, with connecting maps given by suitable
\rho_{s}
.

As discussed in Lemma
3.3
, there is a canonical way to “transform” the diagram
F\circ p
into a new diagram with surjective transition maps but with the same inverse limit. Indeed, one defines:

K_{g}=\bar{K}=\bigcap_{s\in S}\rho_{s}(K)\quad\text{and}\quad\bar{\rho}_{s}=(\rho_{s})_{\restriction\bar{K}}\colon K_{g}\to K_{gs},

for all
g\in G
and
s\in S
. Then, the inverse system
{\mathfrak{K}}=\{(K_{g},\bar{\rho}_{s}\colon K_{g}\to K_{gs}):g\in G,\ s\in S\}
is the same that we have used in Lemma
3.12
and, therefore, by the above discussion and by Lemmas
3.3
and
3.12
, we obtain the desired isomorphisms:

K^{*}=\varprojlim{}_{(G,\leq_{S})}{\mathfrak{K}}\cong\varprojlim(F\circ p)\cong\iota_{*}(F)(\star).

4
The Topological Addition Theorem

The aim of this section is to prove the Topological Addition Theorem, as stated in the introduction.
First we prove it for actions of amenable groups; we then deduce the general case by using the invariance of the topological entropy under Ore colocalization from §
3.3
.

4.1
Properties of open covers of compact spaces related to projections

In this subsection we deal with an amenable group
G
, compact spaces
K
and
Q
, two right
G
-actions
K\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 7.0pt$\rho$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowleft\ $}}G
and
Q\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 7.0pt$\rho_{Q}$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowleft\ $}}G
, and a
G
-equivariant surjective continuous map
\pi\colon K\to Q
. To simplify our heavy notations, we let, for each
{\cal U}\in\mathrm{cov}(K)
,
{\cal W}\in\mathrm{cov}(Q)
and
F\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{fin}}(G)
,

{\cal U}_{F}={\cal U}_{\rho,F}=\bigvee_{f\in F}\rho_{f}^{-1}({\cal U})\quad\text{and}\quad{\cal W}_{F}={\cal W}_{\rho_{Q},F}=\bigvee_{f\in F}(\rho_{Q})_{f}^{-1}({\cal W});

moreover, for
f\in F
,
{\cal U}_{f}=\rho_{f}^{-1}({\cal U})
and
{\cal W}_{f}=(\rho_{Q})_{f}^{-1}({\cal W})
.

The reader may keep in mind that the properties established in this section are needed in §
4.3
for the specific case when
K\in{\mathbf{CompGr}}
,
H
is a closed
G
-invariant subgroup of
K
,
Q=K/H
is the left cosets space and
\pi\colon K\to Q
is the
G
-equivariant projection. Nevertheless, we keep the more general setting here since we feel that also appropriate counterparts of Propositions
4.9
and
4.10
for compact spaces can be proved using uniform covers in place of even ones in that more general setting.

Definition 4.1
.

Let
K
and
Q
be compact spaces,
\pi\colon K\to Q
a surjective continuous map, and
{\cal W}\in\mathrm{cov}(Q)
. Define:

N({\cal U}|{\cal W})=\sup_{W\in{\cal W}}N_{\pi^{-1}(W)}({\cal U})\quad\text{and}\quad N({\cal U}|\pi)=\sup_{q\in Q}N_{\pi^{-1}(q)}({\cal U}).

When
K
is a compact group and
Q=K/H
, where
H
is a closed subgroup of
K
, we get
N({\cal U}|\pi)=\sup_{k\in K}N_{kH}({\cal U})
.

Proposition 4.2
.

In the above setting, the following properties of
N(-|-)
hold true:

(1)

1=N({\cal U}_{0}|{\cal W})\leq N({\cal U}_{1}|{\cal W})\leq N({\cal U}_{2}|{\cal W})
, if
\{K\}={\cal U}_{0}\preceq{\cal U}_{1}\preceq{\cal U}_{2}
in
\mathrm{cov}(K)
and
{\cal W}\in\mathrm{cov}(Q)
;

(2)

N({\cal U}|{\cal W}_{2})\leq N({\cal U}|{\cal W}_{1})\leq N({\cal U}|{\cal W}_{0})=N({\cal U})
, if
{\cal U}\in\mathrm{cov}(K)
and
\{Q\}={\cal W}_{0}\preceq{\cal W}_{1}\preceq{\cal W}_{2}
in
\mathrm{cov}(Q)
;

(3)

\log N({\cal U})\leq\log N({\cal W})+\log N({\cal U}|{\cal W})
, for all
{\cal U}\in\mathrm{cov}(K)
and
{\cal W}\in\mathrm{cov}(Q)
;

(4)

\log N({\cal U}_{1}\vee{\cal U}_{2}|{\cal W})\leq\log N({\cal U}_{1}|{\cal W})+\log N({\cal U}_{2}|{\cal W})
, for all
{\cal U}_{1},{\cal U}_{2}\in\mathrm{cov}(K)
and
{\cal W}\in\mathrm{cov}(Q)
.

Furthermore,
N(-|-)
has the following relations with the invariant
N(-|\pi)
:

(5)

for each
{\cal U}\in\mathrm{cov}(K)
, there exists
q\in Q
, such that
N({\cal U}|\pi)=N_{\pi^{-1}(q)}({\cal U})
, so
N({\cal U}|{\cal W})\geq N({\cal U}|\pi)
, for all
{\cal U}\in\mathrm{cov}(K)
and
{\cal W}\in\mathrm{cov}(Q)
;

(6)

for every finite subset
\{{\cal U}_{1},\ldots,{\cal U}_{n}\}\subseteq\mathrm{cov}(K)
,
there exists
{\cal W}\in\mathrm{cov}(Q)
such that
N({\cal U}_{t}|{\cal W})=N({\cal U}_{t}|\pi)
for all
t=1,\dots,n
(and so,
N({\cal U}_{t}|{\cal W}^{\prime})=N({\cal U}_{t}|\pi)
for all
{\cal W}\preceq{\cal W}^{\prime}\in\mathrm{cov}(Q)
).

Proof.

(1) Given
W\in{\cal W}
, it is clear that
\pi^{-1}(W)\subseteq K
, so that
N({\cal U}_{0}|{\cal W})=1
. Now, if
{\cal U}_{2}^{\prime}=\{U_{2,j}:{j=1,\dots,n}\}\subseteq{\cal U}_{2}
is a subset such that
{\pi^{-1}(W)}\subseteq\bigcup_{j=1}^{n}U_{2,j}
, there is
{\cal U}_{1}^{\prime}=\{U_{1,j}:{j=1,\dots,n}\}\subseteq{\cal U}_{1}
such that
U_{2,j}\subseteq U_{1,j}
for all
j=1,\dots,n
, and therefore
N_{\pi^{-1}(W)}({\cal U}_{1})\leq N_{\pi^{-1}(W)}({\cal U}_{2})
. As this holds for all
W\in{\cal W}
, we deduce that
N({\cal U}_{1}|{\cal W})\leq N({\cal U}_{2}|{\cal W})
.
Since
{\cal U}_{0}\preceq{\cal U}_{1}
, this also shows that
N({\cal U}_{0}|{\cal W})\leq N({\cal U}_{1}|{\cal W})
.

(2) For each
W\in{\cal W}_{2}
, there exists
W^{\prime}\in{\cal W}_{1}
such that
W\subseteq W^{\prime}
, and therefore,
N_{{\pi^{-1}(W)}}({\cal U})\leq N_{{\pi^{-1}(W^{\prime})}}({\cal U})
. As this holds for all
W\in{\cal W}_{2}
, we deduce that
N({\cal U}|{\cal W}_{2})\leq N({\cal U}|{\cal W}_{1})
. Similarly, as
{\cal W}_{0}\preceq{\cal W}_{1}
, also
N({\cal U}|{\cal W}_{1})\leq N({\cal U}|{\cal W}_{0})
and, by definition,
N({\cal U}|{\cal W}_{0})=N_{\pi^{-1}(Q)}({\cal U})=N_{K}({\cal U})=N({\cal U})
.

(3) Suppose that
N({\cal W})=n
and let
{\cal W}^{\prime}=\{W_{1},\dots,W_{n}\}\subseteq{\cal W}
be a minimal subcover. Choose, for each
k=1,\dots,n
, a minimal subset
{\cal U}_{i}=\{U_{k,1},\dots,U_{k,m_{k}}\}\subseteq{\cal U}
that covers
\pi^{-1}(W_{k})
. Then,

K=\pi^{-1}(Q)=\bigcup_{k=1}^{n}\pi^{-1}(W_{k})=\bigcup_{k=1}^{n}\left(\bigcup_{j=1}^{m_{k}}U_{k,j}\right),

and therefore,
{\cal U}^{\prime}=\bigcup_{k=1}^{n}{\cal U}_{k}
is a subcover of
{\cal U}
, showing that
N({\cal U})\leq|{\cal U}^{\prime}|\leq\sum_{k=1}^{n}m_{k}
. By construction, we have that
m_{k}=N_{\pi^{-1}({W}_{k})}({\cal U})\leq N({\cal U}|{\cal W})
and
n=N({\cal W})
, thus
N({\cal U})\leq n\cdot N({\cal U}|{\cal W})=N({\cal W})\cdot N({\cal U}|{\cal W})
. To conclude, apply logarithms on both sides of the equation.

(4) For each
W\in{\cal W}
, let
{\cal U}_{1,W}\subseteq{\cal U}_{1}
and
{\cal U}_{2,W}\subseteq{\cal U}_{2}
be two minimal subsets that cover
\pi^{-1}(W)
. Then, by definition,
{\cal U}_{1,W}\vee{\cal U}_{2,W}=\{U_{1}\cap U_{2}:U_{1}\in{\cal U}_{1,W},\ U_{2}\in{\cal U}_{2,W}\}
is a subset of
{\cal U}_{1}\vee{\cal U}_{2}
that covers
\pi^{-1}(W)
. In particular,
N_{\pi^{-1}(W)}({\cal U}_{1})\cdot N_{\pi^{-1}(W)}({\cal U}_{2})=|{\cal U}_{1,W}|\cdot|{\cal U}_{2,W}|\geq|{\cal U}_{1,W}\vee{\cal U}_{2,W}|\geq N_{\pi^{-1}(W)}({\cal U}_{1}\vee{\cal U}_{2})
. As this holds for all
W\in{\cal W}
, we deduce that
N({\cal U}_{1}\vee{\cal U}_{2}|{\cal W})\leq N({\cal U}_{1}|{\cal W})\cdot N({\cal U}_{2}|{\cal W})
. To conclude, just take the logarithm of this inequality.

(5) Given
{\cal U}\in\mathrm{cov}(K)
, the set
\{N_{\pi^{-1}(q)}({\cal U}):q\in Q\}\subseteq\{0,\dots,N({\cal U})\}
is a finite subset of
{\mathbb{N}}
, so its supremum
N({\cal U}|\pi)
is a maximum, and we can choose
q\in Q
such that
N({\cal U}|\pi)=N_{\pi^{-1}(q)}({\cal U})
. Choose also
W\in{\cal W}
such that
q\in W
, then
\pi^{-1}(q)\subseteq\pi^{-1}(W)
, and so
N({\cal U}|{\cal W})\geq N_{\pi^{-1}(W)}({\cal U})\geq N_{\pi^{-1}(q)}({\cal U})=N({\cal U}|\pi)
.

(6) Consider first the case
n=1
, i.e., we have to see that for each
{\cal U}\in\mathrm{cov}(K)
, there exists
{\cal W}\in\mathrm{cov}(Q)
such that
N({\cal U}|{\cal W})=N({\cal U}|\pi)
.
Let
{\cal U}\in\mathrm{cov}(K)
. For each
q\in Q
, choose a subset
{\cal U}_{q}\subseteq{\cal U}
such that
|{\cal U}_{q}|=N_{\pi^{-1}(q)}({\cal U})
and

\pi^{-1}(q)\subseteq\bigcup{\cal U}_{q}=A_{q}
; clearly,
A_{q}\subseteq K
is open. We claim that there is an open neighborhood
W_{q}
of
q\in Q
such that

\pi^{-1}(q)\subseteq\pi^{-1}(W_{q})\subseteq A_{q}.

(4.1)

Indeed, being
Q
a regular space, there is a family of open neighborhoods
\{O_{i}:i\in I\}
of
q
such that

\bigcap_{i\in I}\mathrm{cl}(O_{i})=\{q\}
. Then,
\bigcap_{i\in I}\pi^{-1}(\mathrm{cl}(O_{i}))=\pi^{-1}(q)\subseteq A_{q}
.
Since
K
is compact,
A_{q}
is open and
\pi^{-1}(\mathrm{cl}(O_{i}))
is closed, for all
i\in I
, there is a finite subset
J\subseteq I
such that
\pi^{-1}(q)\subseteq\bigcap_{i\in J}\pi^{-1}(\mathrm{cl}(O_{i}))\subseteq A_{q}
. Then,
W_{q}=\bigcap_{i\in J}O_{i}
is an open neighborhood of
q
in
Q
that satisfies (
4.1
).
Let
{\cal W}=\{W_{q}\colon q\in Q\}\in\mathrm{cov}(Q)
. Then, for each
q\in Q
,

N_{\pi^{-1}(W_{q})}({\cal U})\leq N_{A_{q}}({\cal U})\leq|{\cal U}_{q}|=N_{\pi^{-1}(q)}({\cal U})\leq N({\cal U}|\pi).

As this holds for all
q\in Q
, we deduce that
N({\cal U}|{\cal W})\leq N{({\cal U}|\pi)}
. Equality holds by item (5).

Now we consider the general case. By the first part of the argument and item (2), for each
t=1,\dots,n
, there exists
{\cal W}_{t}\in\mathrm{cov}(Q)
such that
N({\cal U}_{t}|{\cal W}^{\prime})=N({\cal U}_{t}|\pi)
, for any
{\cal W}^{\prime}
that refines
{\cal W}_{t}
. Hence, letting
{\cal W}=\bigvee_{t=1}^{n}{\cal W}_{t}
, we have that

N({\cal U}_{t}|{\cal W})=N({\cal U}_{t}|\pi)
for all
t=1,\dots,n
.

For the last assertion, combine with (2) and (5).
∎

Consider the following consequences of Proposition
4.2
:

Corollary 4.3
.

Let
G
be an amenable group,
K
and
Q
compact spaces,
K\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 7.0pt$\rho$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowleft\ $}}G
and
Q\mathrlap{\scalebox{.73}{\raisebox{5.8125pt}{\hskip 7.0pt$\rho_{Q}$}}}{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{$\ \curvearrowleft\ $}}G
two right
G
-actions, and suppose that the surjective continuous map
\pi\colon K\to Q
is
G
-equivariant. Let
{\cal U}\in\mathrm{cov}(K)
and
{\cal W}\in\mathrm{cov}(Q)
. Then:

(1)

N({\cal U}_{gF}|\pi)=N({\cal U}_{F}|\pi)
and
N({\cal U}_{gF_{1}}|{\cal W}_{F_{2}})=N({\cal U}_{F_{1}}|{\cal W}_{g^{-1}F_{2}})
, for all
F,F_{1},F_{2}\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{fin}}(G)
and
g\in G
;

(2)

\log N({\cal U}_{F}|{\cal W})\leq|F|\cdot\log N({\cal U})
, for all
F\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{fin}}(G)
;

(3)

\log N({\cal U}_{F}|{\cal W}_{F})\leq\log N({\cal U}_{F_{1}}|{\cal W}_{F})+\log N({\cal U}_{F_{2}}|{\cal W}_{F})\leq\log N({\cal U}_{F_{1}}|{\cal W}_{F_{1}})+\log N({\cal U}_{F_{2}}|{\cal W}_{F_{2}})
, for all
F_{1},F_{2}\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{fin}}(G)
and
F=F_{1}\cup F_{2}
;

(4)

\log N({\cal U}_{F}|\pi)\leq\log N({\cal U}_{F_{1}}|\pi)+\log N({\cal U}_{F_{2}}|\pi)
, for all
F_{1},F_{2},F=F_{1}\cup F_{2}\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{fin}}(G)
.

Proof.

(1) First we prove the second assertion: take the bijection
{\cal W}_{F_{2}}\to{\cal W}_{g^{-1}F_{2}}
given by
W\mapsto(\rho_{Q})_{g}(W)
, for all
W
in
{\cal W}_{F_{2}}
. Furthermore, given
W\in{\cal W}_{F_{2}}
,
{\cal U}^{\prime}\subseteq{\cal U}_{gF_{1}}
covers
\pi^{-1}(W)
if and only if
{\cal U}^{\prime\prime}=\rho_{g}({\cal U}^{\prime})=\{\rho_{g}(U):U\in{\cal U}^{\prime}\}\subseteq{\cal U}_{F_{1}}
covers
\pi^{-1}((\rho_{Q})_{g}(W))
. In fact,

\pi^{-1}(W)\subseteq\bigcup{\cal U}^{\prime}\quad\text{if and only if}\quad\pi^{-1}((\rho_{Q})_{g}(W))=\rho_{g}(\pi^{-1}(W))\subseteq\rho_{g}\left(\bigcup_{U\in{\cal U}^{\prime}}U\right)=\bigcup_{U\in{\cal U}^{\prime}}\rho_{g}(U)=\bigcup{\cal U}^{\prime\prime}.

The proof of the first assertion follows the same line. In fact, the map
{\cal U}_{F}\to{\cal U}_{gF}
such that
U\mapsto\rho_{g^{-1}}(U)
, for all
U\in{\cal U}_{F}
, is a bijection. Moreover, given
q\in Q
, a subset
{\cal U}^{\prime}\subseteq{\cal U}_{F}
covers
\pi^{-1}(q)
if and only if
{\cal U}^{\prime\prime}=\rho_{g^{-1}}({\cal U}^{\prime})\subseteq{\cal U}_{gF}
covers
\pi^{-1}((\rho_{Q})_{g^{-1}}(q))
; and
(\rho_{Q})_{g^{-1}}\colon Q\to Q
is a bijection.

(2) By Proposition
4.2
(4), we deduce that
\log N({\cal U}_{F}|{\cal W})\leq\sum_{f\in F}\log N({\cal U}_{f}|{\cal W})
. Furthermore, by part (1) and Proposition
4.2
(2),
N({\cal U}_{f}|{\cal W})=N({\cal U}|{\cal W}_{f^{-1}})\leq N({\cal U})
, for all
f\in F
. Hence,

\log N({\cal U}_{F}|{\cal W})\leq\sum_{f\in F}\log N({\cal U}|{\cal W}_{f^{-1}})\leq|F|\cdot\log N({\cal U}).

(3) The statement follows by the following formula, where we apply Proposition
4.2
(4) and (2) in the inequalities:

\log N({\cal U}_{F}|{\cal W}_{F})=\log N({\cal U}_{F_{1}}\vee{\cal U}_{F_{2}}|{\cal W}_{F})\leq\log N({\cal U}_{F_{1}}|{\cal W}_{F})+\log N({\cal U}_{F_{2}}|{\cal W}_{F})\leq\log N({\cal U}_{F_{1}}|{\cal W}_{F_{1}})+\log N({\cal U}_{F_{2}}|{\cal W}_{F_{2}}).

(4) By Proposition
4.2
(6), there exists
{\cal W}\in\mathrm{cov}(Q)
such that
N({\cal U}_{F}|{\cal W})=N({\cal U}_{F}|\pi)
,
N({\cal U}_{F_{1}}|{\cal W})=N({\cal U}_{F_{1}}|\pi)
and
N({\cal U}_{F_{2}}|{\cal W})=N({\cal U}_{F_{2}}|\pi)
. Hence, making use of Proposition
4.2
(4), we get

\log N({\cal U}_{F}|\pi)=\log N({\cal U}_{F}|{\cal W})=\log N({\cal U}_{F_{1}}\vee{\cal U}_{F_{2}}|{\cal W})\leq\log N({\cal U}_{F_{1}}|{\cal W})+\log N({\cal U}_{F_{2}}|{\cal W})=\log N({\cal U}_{F_{1}}|\pi)+\log N({\cal U}_{F_{2}}|\pi).\qed

The above corollary implies that, given
{\cal U}\in\mathrm{cov}(K)
and
{\cal W}\in\mathrm{cov}(Q)
, the two functions
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{fin}}(G)\to{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}
, defined by

F\mapsto\log N({\cal U}_{F}|{\cal W}_{F})\ \mbox{ and }\ F\mapsto\log N({\cal U}_{F}|\pi),

are both subadditive and left-invariant. So, they satisfy the hypotheses of the Ornstein-Weiss Lemma, hence, for every right Følner net
{\mathfrak{s}}=\{F_{i}\}_{i\in I}
for
G
, these two nets converge:

\left\{\frac{\log N({\cal U}_{F_{i}}|\pi)}{|F_{i}|}\right\}_{i\in I}\quad\text{and}\quad\left\{\frac{\log N({\cal U}_{F_{i}}|{\cal W}_{F_{i}})}{|F_{i}|}\right\}_{i\in I}.

Lemma 4.4
.

In the above setting, let
{\mathfrak{s}}=\{F_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be a right Følner net for
G
and
\varepsilon>0
. Then, there exists
\bar{\imath}\in I
such that

\log N({\cal U}_{F_{j}}|\pi)\leq\frac{|F_{j}|}{|F_{i}|}\log N({\cal U}_{F_{i}}|\pi)+\varepsilon\ |F_{j}|,\ \ \text{for all $i,j\geq\bar{\imath}^{\prime}$ in $I$}.

(4.2)

Proof.

Being convergent,
\{\log N({\cal U}_{F_{k}}|\pi)/|F_{k}|\}_{k\in I}
is a Cauchy net in
{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}
. Therefore, there is some
\bar{\imath}\in I
such that

\left|\frac{\log N({\cal U}_{F_{j}}|\pi)}{|F_{j}|}-\frac{\log N({\cal U}_{F_{i}}|\pi)}{|F_{i}|}\right|\leq\varepsilon,\ \ \text{for all $\bar{\imath}\leq i,j\in I$.}

We then obtain the following inequality, which clearly implies (
4.2
):
